In this subsection, we discuss about the simplest interacting scalar field theory, namely, a single massless scalar field with a
\Phi^{4}
interaction. The Lagrangian for such scalar field is expressed as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}\;=\;\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\mu}\Phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{24}{g^{2}}\Phi^{4}+\Delta{\mathcal{L}}\;,

(4.1)

where
g
denotes the coupling constant, and
\Delta{\mathcal{L}}
represents the renormalised counterterms.
The Lagrangian density in (
4.1
) can be divided into a free part and an interacting part as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\mu}\Phi)^{2}\;\hskip 36.135pt\Rightarrow\hskip 93.95122pt\,{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{./figs/scalar_propagator.pdf}}

(4.2)

\displaystyle\vskip 0.0pt{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm int}\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{1}{24}g^{2}\Phi^{4}\;\hskip 28.90755pt\Rightarrow\hskip 180.67499pt{}\,\,\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.4}]{./figs/scalar_4point.pdf}}}\,\,\,\,\,,

(4.3)

where
P\equiv(p_{0},\bm{\vec{p}})
is the Euclidean four-momentum. The Euclidean energy
p_{0}
takes discrete values:
p_{0}=2n\pi iT
for bosons and
p_{0}=(2n+1)\pi iT
for fermions, where
n
is an integer. The radiative corrections are then calculated in a loop expansion, which is equivalent to a power series in
g^{2}
. It becomes apparent that the perturbative expansion fails at finite temperature, and the weak-coupling expansion transforms into an expansion in
g
rather than
g^{2n}
, where
n
is the number of loops.

4.2  Self-Energy

We will first calculate the self-energy by evaluating the relevant diagrams.

4.2.1  One-loop self-energy

The Feynman diagram that contributes to the 1-loop self-energy is the tadpole diagram as shown in Fig.
4.1
.
The one-loop diagram is independent of the external momentum and the resulting integral expression is

\displaystyle\Pi^{(1)}\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{1}{2}g^{2}\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{P}\frac{1}{P^{2}}\;=\frac{1}{24}g^{2}T^{2}\;\equiv(m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}\,,

(4.4)

where the superscript indicates the number of loops.

Figure 4.1
:
One-loop scalar self-energy graph.

Equation (
4.4
) represents the leading order thermal mass of scalar field with
\Phi^{4}
interaction. The sum-integral over
P
represents a summation over Matsubara frequencies and integration of spatial momenta in
d=3-2\epsilon
dimensions. The sum-integral in Eq. (
4.4
) has ultraviolet power divergences that are set to zero in dimensional regularization. We are then left with the finite result (
4.4
), indicating that thermal fluctuations induce a mass for the scalar field of the order
gT
. The thermal mass
m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}}
is analogous to the Debye mass, well-known in non-relativistic QED plasma.

4.2.2  Two-loop self-energy

There are two 2-loop diagrams as shown in Fig.
4.2
The first one is known as double-bubble diagram whereas the second one is known as sunset diagram. We first consider the double-bubble diagram in Fig.
4.2
.

Figure 4.2
:
Double-bubble and sunset diagrams in 2-loop scalar self-energy.

This diagram is also independent of the external momentum and gives the following sum-integral

\displaystyle\Pi^{(2)}\;=\;-\frac{1}{4}g^{4}\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{PQ}\frac{1}{P^{2}}\frac{1}{Q^{4}}\;.

(4.5)

This integral is IR divergent. The problem stems from the middle loop with two propagators. In order to isolate the source of the divergence, we look at the contribution from the zeroth Matsubara mode to the
Q
integration

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}g^{4}\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{P}\frac{1}{P^{2}}T\int_{\bm{q}}\frac{1}{q^{4}}=-\frac{1}{4}g^{4}\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{P}\frac{1}{P^{2}}T\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dq}{q^{2}}\,.

(4.6)

The integral over
q
in (
4.6
) behaves like
1/q^{2}
and quadratic IR divergent as
q\rightarrow 0
. This infrared divergence indicates that naive perturbation theory breaks down at finite temperature. However, one can cure this IR divergence by
``
resumming” the leading order correction into the propagator for the
Q
propagator (see subsec
4.4
) as

\displaystyle\Delta^{\star}(Q)\;=\;\frac{1}{Q^{2}-(m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}\;,

(4.7)

where
m_{\rm{th}}^{\rm s}
can be obtained from (
4.4
) as
m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}}=gT/\sqrt{24}\ll T
. The mass term in the propagator (
4.7
) provides an IR cutoff of order
gT
. The contribution from (
4.6
) would then be

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}g^{4}\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{P}\frac{1}{P^{2}}T\int_{\bf q}\frac{1}{(q^{2}+(m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2})^{2}}\;=\;-\frac{1}{4}g^{4}\left(\frac{T^{2}}{12}\right)\left(\frac{T}{8\pi m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}}}\right)+{\mathcal{O}}\left(g^{4}m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}}T\right)\;.

(4.8)

Since
m\sim gT
, the leading term is
{\mathcal{O}}(g^{3})
and the subleading term is
{\mathcal{O}}(g^{5})
. We now note that the leading contribution from the double-bubble contributes is in the order
g^{3}T^{2}
to the self-energy but not at order
g^{4}T^{2}
. This is different than the BPT in which all terms are even powers of the form
g^{2n}
.

We now note that the
``
sunset diagram” is IR finite and contributes at order
g^{4}
and, hence, is subleading.

4.2.3  Three-loop self-energy

On the other hand, the three-loop diagram as shown in left panel of Fig.
4.3
is known as snowman graph and contributes at the order
g^{4}T^{2}
and hence subleading.

Figure 4.3
:
Left panel
: Subleading order three-loop self-energy diagram;
Right panel
: Leading order three-bubble diagram in scalar self-energy.

Next we consider three-bubble diagram as shown in the right panel of Fig.
4.3
and the leading term is
{\mathcal{O}}(g^{3})
but not of the
{\mathcal{O}}(g^{6})
as one might have expected from BPT.
Similarly, one can show that the diagrams with any number of bubbles like
Fig.
4.3

are all of
{\mathcal{O}}(g^{3})
.

4.3  Resummation of Bubble Diagrams in HTL Approximation

As seen from previous subsecetions that the BPT, clearly, breaks down since the
{\mathcal{O}}(g^{3})
correction to the self-energy receives contributions from all loop orders. This is because going to higher loops orders one identifies a class of diagrams that all contribute at
{\mathcal{O}}(g^{3})
. They are the so-called
``
bubblegraphs” which are distinct from, e.g. the sunset graph or the snowman graph. Therefore, one only needs to resum a subset of all possible bubble graphs as shown in Fig.
4.4
in order to obtain a consistent expansion in
g
.

Figure 4.4
:
Resummation of bubble diagrams. The diagram (
a
) is of
{\mathcal{O}}(g^{2})
whereas
those in (
b
), (
c
) and higher order bubble graphs are of
{\mathcal{O}}(g^{3})
.

Summing the entire series one obtain the effective 1-loop scalar self-energy as

\displaystyle\Pi^{(1)\star}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{g^{2}}{24}T^{2}\left[1-\frac{g\sqrt{6}}{4\pi}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(g^{2}\right)\right]\;.

(4.9)

The
{\mathcal{O}}(g^{2})
corresponds to the 1-loop self-energy diagram in Fig.
4.4
(a) and the
{\mathcal{O}}(g^{3})
corresponds to the summation of the bubble diagrams in Fig.
4.4
(b), Fig.
4.4
(c) and higher order bubble diagrams.

It turns out that one can also obtain the same result by simply putting the massive thermal effective propagator as given in (
4.7
) in 1-loop self-energy as given in the left hand side of Fig.
4.4
. Then one obtains

\displaystyle\Pi^{(1)\star}\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{1}{2}g^{2}\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{P}\frac{1}{P^{2}-(m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}g^{2}\Big[-T\int_{\bf p}\frac{1}{p^{2}+(m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}+\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{P}^{\prime}\frac{1}{P^{2}}+{\mathcal{O}}\left((m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}\right)\Big]

(4.10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{g^{2}}{24}T^{2}\left[1-\frac{g\sqrt{6}}{4\pi}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(g^{2}\right)\right]\;,

where the prime on the sum-integral indicates that we have excluded the
n=0
mode from the sum over the Matsubara frequencies. The order
g^{3}
corresponds to the summation of the bubble diagrams in Fig.
4.4
, which is obtained by expanding the effective propagator in (
4.7
) around
m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}}=0
. Thus by taking the thermal mass into account, one is resumming an infinite set of bubble diagrams from all orders in perturbation theory.

4.4  Resummed Scalar Propagator in HTL Approximation

We have found in subsec
4.3
that, at the level of the propagator (2-point function), there is an infinite set of graphs that contribute at next-to-leading order, i.e., the
{\mathcal{O}}(g^{3})
in the scalar self-energy. This continues as one proceeds to higher orders. The summation of these graphs can be accomplished in a more straightforward manner by using the HTL resummed propagator as shown in Fig.
4.5
.

Figure 4.5
:
Resummation of effective scalar propagator in 1-loop scalar self-energy.

The full propagator in 1-loop resummation is given by Dyson-Schwinger equation following Fig.
4.5
as

\Delta^{\star}=\Delta+\Delta\Pi^{(1)}\Delta^{\star}\,.

Solving this one obtains the effective scalar propagator as

\displaystyle\Delta^{\star}(P)\;

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\frac{1}{P^{2}-\Pi^{(1)}}\;.

(4.11)

The bare one loop scalar self-energy in (
4.4
) is the first example of HTL approximation. To see this, we consider the bare one-loop self-energy in Fig.
4.1
can be written from the first line of (
4.4
) as

\displaystyle\Pi^{(1)}\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{1}{2}g^{2}\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{P}\frac{1}{P^{2}}\;=\;\frac{g^{2}}{2}T\sum_{p_{0}=2\pi inT}\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{p_{0}^{2}-\bm{\vec{p}}^{2}}\,\,,

(4.12)

Figure 4.6
:
HTL contribution dominates the one-loop scalar self-energy at the hard loop momenta scale
p\sim T
.

which after bosonic Matsubara frequency sum transforms into an integral involving the Bose-Einstein distribution function,
n_{B}(p)
, as

\displaystyle\Pi^{(1)}\!\!

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle\frac{g^{2}}{T}\int_{0}^{\infty}dp\,p^{2}\,n_{B}(p)\,.

(4.13)

The integral in (
4.13
) is found to dominate at hard loop momenta,
p\sim T
which is displayed in Fig.
4.6
.
Now, the effective propagator in HTL can now be written as

\displaystyle\Delta^{\star}(P)\;=\;\frac{1}{P^{2}-(m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}\;.\hfill

(4.14)

where
m
can be obtained from (
4.4
) as
m_{\rm{th}}^{s}=gT/\sqrt{24}
. If the momenta of the propagator are of order
T
or
hard
, it is evident that the thermal mass acts as a perturbation and can be neglected. However, when the momenta of the propagator are of order
gT
or
soft
, the thermal mass becomes as significant as the bare inverse propagator and cannot be ignored. Therefore, by considering the thermal mass, one effectively resums an infinite set of diagrams in scalar self-energy from all orders of perturbation theory as described in (
4.10
) in subsec
4.3
.

4.5
N
-point Functions in
\Phi^{4}
-Theory in HTL Approximation

In the preceding section, we explored two-point function in scalar field theory. Upon calculating higher-order
N
-point functions in scalar theory, it becomes evident that the one-loop correction to the four-point function at high temperatures behaves
[
76
]
as

\Gamma^{(4)}\;\propto\;g^{4}\log\left(T/p\right)\;,

where
p
represents the external spatial momentum. Consequently, the loop correction to the four-point function increases logarithmically with temperature,
T
. Hence, it is always down by
g^{4}\log(1/g)
as the external momentum
p
is soft in HTL approximation, suggesting that employing a bare vertex suffices.

More generally, one finds that the only HTL in scalar
\Phi^{4}
-field theory is the tadpole diagram in Fig.
4.1
and resummation is taken care of by including the thermal mass in the propagator in subsec
4.4
. However, in gauge theories, the scenario is much more complicated than the scalar theory as we shall discuss in the next two sections
5
and
6
, respectively.

4.6  HTL Improved Scalar Lagrangian

In the previous subsection
4.2
, we have seen that when evaluating higher order vacuum diagrams, one encounters divergences in the IR regime. These divergences are encountered by using only the propagators with zero Matsubara frequencies which do not have an IR cutoff of order
T
. This problem can be solved by using the thermal mass,
m
in (
4.4
) as a IR cutoff, which is then incorporated into the theory by changing the Lagrangian in (
4.1
) to

{\mathcal{L}}_{\textrm{eff}}={\mathcal{L}}_{0}+{\mathcal{L}}_{\textrm{int}}+\Delta{\mathcal{L}}\,,

where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\mu}\Phi)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}{(m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}\Phi^{2}\,,

(4.15a)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\textrm{int}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{24}{g^{2}}\Phi^{4}+\frac{1}{2}{(m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}\Phi^{2}\,.

(4.15b)

We note that the physics of the problem at hand do not change when this is done, since the same term is added and subtracted in the Lagrangian. However, in the interaction Lagrangian in (
4.15b
), the
(m^{\textrm{s}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}
-term can also be interpreted as an interaction that couples two scalar fields. Due to this it is expected that in addition to diagrams consisting of loops and four-point vertices, new diagrams will be encountered.

5  Quantum Electrodynamics (QED)

Quantum electrodynamics (QED) is an abelian gauge theory. The symmetry group is
U(1)
abelian group, which is also a commutative group. In QED, the interaction between two spin
1/2
fermionic fields is mediated by electromagnetic field photon, which is a gauge field.

5.1  Lagrangian

The QED Lagrangian density describing massless fermions, electromagnetic field and
interaction between them as

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\mbox{\tiny{QED}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2\xi}(\partial_{\mu}A^{\mu})^{2}+{\bar{\psi}}i\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi-e\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi A_{\mu}+\delta{\mathcal{L}}_{\textrm{\tiny QED}},

(5.1)

where
\psi
and
\bar{\psi}
are Dirac fields.
F_{\mu\nu}
is known as electromagnetic field tensor and is given as

F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu},

This is a gauge invariant quantity and also antisymmetric,
F_{\mu\nu}=-F_{\nu\mu}
, under the exchange of the Lorentz
indices
\mu\leftrightarrow\nu
.
A_{\mu}
is the gauge field (photon) and
\xi
is the gauge fixing parameter which takes
1
in Feynman gauge and
0
in Landau gauge.
\delta{\mathcal{L}}_{\textrm{\tiny QED}}
is the renormalised counter terms.

5.1.1  Feynman rules

We note the following from Eq. (
5.1
):

\bullet

The first term corresponds to the free Lagrangian density of a gauge field (photon).

\bullet

The second one corresponds to the gauge fixing term.

\bullet

The first and the second terms together lead to a free photon propagator as

\displaystyle\hskip 173.44756pt\Rightarrow\,\,\,\,\,\,iD_{0}^{\mu\nu}(P)=\frac{i}{P^{2}}\left[-\eta^{\mu\nu}+\left(1-\xi\right)\frac{P^{\mu}P^{\nu}}{P^{2}}\right].

(5.2)

\bullet

The third term is the Lagrangian density that describes the free propagation of a massless fermion (electron). The free
massless electron propagator reads as

\displaystyle\hskip 173.44756pt\Rightarrow\,\,\,\,\,iS_{0}(P)=\frac{i}{P\!\!\!\!/\penalty}\,.

(5.3)

\bullet

The fourth term arises from the local
U(1)
gauge symmetry and represents to the interaction Lagrangian density of fermionic and gauge field, where photon field interact with electron field via the dimensionless coupling parameter
e
. By employing this interaction in perturbative theory, one can compute Feynman diagrams for the theory. The electron-photon vertex in QED is represented as

\displaystyle\hskip 21.68121pt\Rightarrow\,\,\,-ie\gamma^{\mu}.

(5.4)

5.2  One-loop Electron Self-energy and Structure Functions in HTL approximation

Using Feynman rules defined in subsec
5.1.1
the QED one-loop electron self-energy in Fig.
5.1
can be written as

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\textrm{e}}(P)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-T\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{\{K\}}(-ie\gamma_{\mu})\frac{i\not{K}}{K^{2}}(-ie\gamma_{\nu})\frac{-i\eta^{\mu\nu}}{(P-K)^{2}}=-2e^{2}T\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{\{K\}}\frac{\not{K}}{K^{2}\ Q^{2}}\,,

(5.5)

Figure 5.1
:
One-loop electron self-energy diagram.

where
Q=(P-K)
and
\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{\{K\}}
is a fermionic sum-integral and
e
is the dimensionless coupling constant in QED. The superscript
e
denotes electron’s self-energy. The detail calculations have done in a review in Ref.
[
5
]
by one of us. We will just quote the final
expression for relevant quantities.

Using Eq. (
5.5
) in Eq. (
2.51
), the structure functions
[
5
]
in the rest frame of the heat bath within HTL approximation can be written as

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{\textrm{e}}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle=-\frac{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{p^{2}}\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\frac{{\bm{{\vec{p}}\cdot{\hat{k}}}}}{\omega-{\bm{\vec{p}\cdot\hat{k}}}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{p^{2}}\left\langle\frac{{\bm{{\vec{p}}\cdot{\hat{k}}}}}{P\cdot\hat{K}}\right\rangle_{\bf\hat{k}}=\,\frac{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{p^{2}}\left[1-\frac{\omega}{2p}\ln\left(\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}\right)\right]\,,

(5.6a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\textrm{e}}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle\,=\frac{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{p^{2}}\left\langle\frac{(P\cdot u)(\bm{\vec{p}\cdot\hat{k}})-p^{2}}{P\cdot\hat{K}}\right\rangle_{\bf\hat{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{p}\left[-\frac{\omega}{p}+\left(\frac{\omega^{2}}{p^{2}}-1\right)\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}\right)\right]\,.

(5.6b)

where
{\hat{K}}=(1,\bm{\hat{k}})
is a light like vector, the angular braces represent the average over the directions specified by the light like vector
{\hat{K}}
and the thermal mass of the electron is obtained as

\displaystyle(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}=\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}\int kdk\left[n_{F}^{+}(k)+n_{F}^{-}(k)+2n_{B}(k)\right]=\frac{e^{2}T^{2}}{8}\left[1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{\pi^{2}T^{2}}\right]\,.

(5.7)

The electron self-energy in the rest frame of the heat bath can be written
[
5
]
within HTL approximation from Eq. (
5.5
) as

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\textrm{e}}(P)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}\left\langle\frac{\hat{\not{K}}}{P\cdot\hat{K}}\right\rangle_{\bf\hat{k}}\,=\frac{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{2p}\ln\left(\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}\right)\gamma_{0}+\frac{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{p}\left[1-\frac{\omega}{2p}\ln\left(\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}\right)\right]\left(\vec{\bm{\gamma}}\cdot\bm{\hat{p}}\right)\,.

(5.8)

This result can also be obtained using Eq. (
5.6a
) and (
5.6b
) in (
2.50
) in the rest frame of the heat bath.

5.3  Effective Electron Propagator and Collective Modes in HTL Approximation

One can write the effective electron propagator from Eq. (
2.63
) in HTL approximation in presence of a thermal medium as

\displaystyle S^{\textrm{e}}(P)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\gamma_{0}-\vec{\bm{\gamma}}\cdot\hat{\bm{p}}}{{\cal D}^{\textrm{e}}_{+}(\omega,p)}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\gamma_{0}+\vec{\bm{\gamma}}\cdot\bm{\hat{p}}}{{\cal D}^{\textrm{e}}_{-}(\omega,p)}.

(5.9)

We can obtain
{\cal D}^{\textrm{e}}_{\pm}(\omega,p)
by combining Eqs. (
2.57
), (
5.6a
) and (
5.6a
) as

\displaystyle{\cal D}^{\textrm{e}}_{\pm}(\omega,p)=\omega\mp p-\frac{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{p}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(1\mp\frac{\omega}{p}\right)\ln\left(\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}\right)\pm 1\right]\,.

(5.10)

Figure 5.2
:
Plot of quasiparticles dispersion in HTL approximation.

While the effective propagator in (
5.9
) exhibits chiral symmetry, the poles of
{\cal D}^{\textrm{e}}_{\pm}(\omega,p)
do not occur at light cone,
\omega=\pm p
. This indicates that the poles of the effective fermion propagator are located away from the light cone in the timelike domain. This discrepancy arises due to the additional term
{\cal B}\gamma_{0}
appearing in self-energy in (
2.50
) due to the breaking of Lorentz invariance at finite temperature
[
50
]
.

The zeros of
{\cal D}^{\textrm{e}}_{\pm}(\omega,p)
define dispersion property of a quark in the thermal bath.
{\cal D}^{\textrm{e}}_{+}(\omega,p)=0
exhibits two ploes at
\omega=\omega_{+}(p)
and
\omega=-\omega_{-}(p)
, while
{\cal D}^{\textrm{e}}_{-}(\omega,p)=0
has two ploes at
\omega=\omega_{-}(p)
and
\omega=-\omega_{+}(p)
. Fig.
5.2
only illustrates the positive energy solutions. A mode with energy
\omega_{+}
signifies the in-medium propagation of a particle excitation, which is a Dirac spinors and eigenstate of
(\gamma_{0}-{\vec{\gamma}}\cdot\bm{\hat{p}})
with chirality to helicity ratio
+1
. On the other hand, there exists a novel long-wavelength mode known as
plasmino
with energy
\omega_{-}
and eigenstate of
(\gamma_{0}+{\vec{\gamma}}\cdot\bm{\hat{p}})
with chirality to helicity ratio
-1
. The
\omega_{-}
branch has a minimum at low momentum and then approaches free dispersion curve at large momentum. In addition,
{\cal D}^{\textrm{e}}_{\pm}(\omega,p)
contains a discontinuous part corresponding to
Landau Damping
(LD) due to the presence of Logarithmic term in Eq.
5.10
.

Below we present the approximate analytic solutions of
\omega_{\pm}(p)
for small and large values of momentum
p
. For small values of momentum (
p\ll m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}
), the dispersion relations are

\displaystyle\omega_{+}(p)

\displaystyle\,\approx\,m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}+\frac{1}{3}p+\frac{1}{3}\frac{p^{2}}{m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}}-\frac{16}{135}\frac{p^{3}}{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}\,,

(5.11a)

\displaystyle\omega_{-}(p)

\displaystyle\,\approx\,m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}-\frac{1}{3}p+\frac{1}{3}\frac{p^{2}}{m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}}+\frac{16}{135}\frac{p^{3}}{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}\,,

(5.11b)

whereas for large values of momentum (
m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}\ll p\ll T
), one obtains

\displaystyle\omega_{+}(p)

\displaystyle\,\approx\,p+\frac{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{p}+\frac{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{4}}{2p^{3}}\ln\frac{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{2p^{2}}+\frac{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{6}}{4p^{5}}\left[\ln^{2}\frac{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{2p^{2}}+\ln\frac{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{2p^{2}}-1\right]\,,

(5.12a)

\displaystyle\omega_{-}(p)

\displaystyle\,\approx\,p+2p\exp\left(-\frac{2p^{2}+(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}\right)\,.

(5.12b)

5.4  Spectral Representation of Fermion Propagator

The in-medium electron propagator (
5.9
) with
{\cal D}^{\rm e}_{\pm}(\omega,p)
are given in Eq. (
5.10
).
According to Eq. (
2.58
),
{\cal D}^{\rm e}_{\pm}(\omega,p)=d_{\pm}(\omega,p)=\omega\pm p
, for free massless case. The corresponding free spectral function can be obtained following the Eq. (
A.5
) as

\displaystyle\rho^{f}_{\pm}(\omega,k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{\pi}{\textrm{Im}}\left.\frac{1}{d_{\pm}(\omega,p)}\right|_{\omega\rightarrow\omega+i\epsilon}=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{\pi}{\textrm{Im}}\frac{1}{\omega\mp p+i\epsilon}=\frac{\delta(\omega\mp p)}{\left|\frac{\ d(\omega\mp p)}{d\omega}\right|}=\delta(\omega\mp p)\,.

(5.13)

As discussed in subsec
5.3
that
{\cal D}^{\rm e}_{\pm}(\omega,p)
has solutions at
\omega_{\pm}(k)
and
-\omega_{\mp}(p)
and a cut part due to space like momentum,
\omega^{2}<p^{2}
. In-medium spectral function corresponding to the effective electron propagator in Eq. (
5.9
) will have both pole and cut contribution as

\rho_{\pm}(\omega,p)=\rho^{\textrm{pole}}_{\pm}(\omega,p)+\rho^{\textrm{cut}}_{\pm}(\omega,p)\,.

The pole part of the spectral function can be obtained following Eq. (
A.5
) as

\displaystyle\rho^{\textrm{pole}}_{\pm}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{\pi}{\textrm{Im}}\left.\frac{1}{{\cal D}^{\rm e}_{\pm}(\omega,p)}\right|_{\omega\rightarrow\omega+i\epsilon}\!\!\!\!\!=\frac{\delta(\omega-\omega_{\pm})}{\left|\frac{d{\cal D^{\rm e}_{\pm}}}{d\omega}\right|_{\omega=\omega_{\pm}}}+\frac{\delta(\omega+\omega_{\mp})}{\left|\frac{d{\cal D^{\rm e}_{\mp}}}{d\omega}\right|_{\omega=-\omega_{\mp}}}

(5.14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\omega^{2}-p^{2})}{2m^{2}_{\textrm{th}}}\left[\delta(\omega-\omega_{\pm})+\delta(\omega+\omega_{\mp})\right]\,.

For
\omega^{2}<p^{2}
, there is a discontinuity in
\ln\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}
as
\ln{y}=\ln\left|y\right|-i\pi\ ,
which leads to the spectral function,
\rho^{\textrm{cut}}_{\pm}(\omega,p)
, corresponding to the discontinuity in
{\cal D}^{\rm e}_{\pm}(\omega,p)
can be obtained from (
A.3
) as

\displaystyle\rho^{\textrm{cut}}_{\pm}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\textrm{Im}\left.\frac{1}{{\cal D}_{\pm}(\omega,p)}\right|_{{\omega\rightarrow\omega+i\epsilon}\atop{\omega<p}}

(5.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{m^{2}_{\textrm{th}}}{2p}\left(1\mp\frac{\omega}{p}\right)\Theta(p^{2}-\omega^{2})}{\left[\omega\mp p-\frac{m^{2}_{\textrm{th}}}{p}\left(\pm 1+\frac{p\mp\omega}{2p}\ln\frac{p+\omega}{p-\omega}\right)\right]^{2}+\left[\frac{\pi m^{2}_{\textrm{th}}}{2p}\left(1\mp\frac{\omega}{p}\right)\right]^{2}}=\beta_{\pm}(\omega,p)\Theta(p^{2}-\omega^{2}).

5.5  Evaluation of
\Pi_{L}
and
\Pi_{T}
from One-loop Photon Self-energy in HTL Approximation

Figure 5.3
:
One-loop photon self-energy diagram.

Using Feyman rules defined in subsec
5.1.1
in Fig.
5.3
and the performing the Dirac trace the photon self - energy can be written as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\gamma}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\!\int\!\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}{\rm Tr}\left[\left(-ie\gamma_{\mu}\right)\frac{i}{\not{K}}(-ie\gamma_{\nu})\frac{i}{\not{K}-\not{P}}\right]

(5.16)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt=-4e^{2}\!\int\!\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{2K_{\mu}K_{\nu}-\eta_{\mu\nu}K^{2}+\eta_{\mu\nu}K\cdot P-(K_{\mu}P_{\nu}+P_{\mu}K_{\nu})}{K^{2}(P-K)^{2}},\,

where superscript
\gamma
in self-energy denotes photon.
Now in HTL approximation one can neglect external soft momentum (
\sim gT
) in the numerator, i.e., one or higher power of
P
. So, we get
[
62
,
63
]
,

\displaystyle\Pi^{\gamma}_{\mu\nu}\approx-8e^{2}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\left[K_{\mu}K_{\nu}\Delta_{F}(K)\Delta_{F}(Q)\right]+4e^{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\Delta_{F}(Q)\,,

(5.17)

where the scalar part of the fermion propagators are
\Delta_{F}(K)={1}/{K^{2}}
and
\Delta_{F}(Q)={1}/{(P-K)^{2}}={1}/{Q^{2}}
with
Q=P-K
. We note that the details calculation within HTL approximation have been done in a review article
[
5
]
by one of us. We will just quote the final expression for various components of photon self-energy. We first write down traced part of the photon self-energy
[
5
]
without vacuum part as

\displaystyle(\Pi^{\gamma})_{\mu}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2e^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}k\ \left[n_{F}^{-}(k)+n_{F}^{-}\right]\ dk=-\frac{e^{2}T^{2}}{3}\left[1+\frac{3\mu^{2}}{\pi^{2}T^{2}}\right]=-(m^{\gamma}_{D})^{2}\,,

(5.18)

where the Debye mass in QED is given as
(m^{\gamma}_{D})^{2}=\frac{e^{2}T^{2}}{3}\left[1+\frac{3\mu^{2}}{\pi^{2}T^{2}}\right]
.
Now we write down the time-time component of photon self-energy
[
5
]
as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\gamma}_{00}(\omega,p)=-4e^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{k^{2}\ dk}{2\pi^{2}}\frac{dn_{F}(k)}{dk}\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\frac{\bm{\vec{p}\cdot\hat{k}}}{\omega-{\bm{\vec{p}\cdot\hat{k}}}}=(m^{\gamma}_{D})^{2}\left\langle\,\frac{\omega-P\cdot{\hat{K}}}{P\cdot{\hat{K}}}\right\rangle_{\bf\hat{k}}\,=(m^{\gamma}_{D})^{2}\left[\frac{\omega}{2p}\ln\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}-1\right]\,.

(5.19)

Following the same procedure, one can obtain the space-space part of the photon self-energy from (
5.17
) as
[
5
]

\displaystyle\Pi^{\gamma}_{ij}(p_{0},p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{2}T^{2}}{3}\,\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\,{\hat{k}}_{i}{\hat{k}}_{j}\left(\frac{p_{0}}{\omega-{\bm{\vec{p}\cdot\hat{k}}}}\right)=(m^{\gamma}_{D})^{2}\left\langle\,{\hat{k}}_{i}{\hat{k}}_{j}\left(\frac{p_{0}}{P\cdot{\hat{K}}}\right)\right\rangle_{\bf\hat{k}}\,,

(5.20)

and also the time-space part as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\gamma}_{0i}(p_{0},p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(m^{\gamma}_{D})^{2}\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\,\left(\frac{p_{0}}{P\cdot{\hat{K}}}\right)\,{\hat{k}}_{i}=(m^{\gamma}_{D})^{2}\left\langle\,\left(\frac{p_{0}}{P\cdot{\hat{K}}}\right){\hat{k}}_{i}\right\rangle_{\bf\hat{k}}\,.

Using Eq. (
5.19
) in Eq. (
2.75
), one obtains the longitudinal component of the photon self-energy as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\gamma}_{L}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{P^{2}}{p^{2}}\Pi^{\gamma}_{00}(\omega,p)=\frac{(m^{\gamma}_{D})^{2}P^{2}}{p^{2}}\left[1-\frac{\omega}{2p}\ln\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}\right]\,.

(5.21)

Now using (
5.18
) and (
5.21
) in (
2.76
), one obtains the transverse component of the photon self-energy as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\gamma}_{T}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[(\Pi^{\gamma})_{\mu}^{\mu}-\Pi^{\gamma}_{L}\right]=-\frac{(m^{\gamma}_{D})^{2}\omega^{2}}{2p^{2}}\left[1+\frac{p^{2}-\omega^{2}}{2\omega p}\ln\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}\right]\,.

(5.22)

We now note that in the IR limit (
\omega\rightarrow 0
), one gets

\displaystyle\lim_{\omega\rightarrow 0}\Pi^{\gamma}_{L}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle\,=\lim_{\omega\rightarrow 0}-\frac{P^{2}}{p^{2}}\Pi^{\gamma}_{00}(\omega,p)=-(m^{\gamma}_{D})^{2}\,,

(5.23a)

\displaystyle\lim_{\omega\rightarrow 0}\Pi^{\gamma}_{T}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle\,=0\,.

(5.23b)

The Eq. (
5.23a
) is the Debye electric screening mass of photon that acts as a IR regulator at the static electric scale (
\sim eT
). Conversely, Eq. (
5.23b
) indicates that there is no screening for magnetic fields as the one-loop photon transverse self-energy in leading order HTL approximation vanishes in the IR limit and provides no magnetic screening mass for photon.

5.6  Effective Photon Propagator and Collective Excitations in HTL Approximation

One write down the HTL effective propagator for photon from Eq. (
2.79
) in presence of thermal medium as

\displaystyle D^{\gamma}_{\mu\nu}=-\left[\frac{\xi}{P^{4}}P_{\mu}P_{\nu}+\frac{1}{P^{2}+\Pi^{\gamma}_{T}}A_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{P^{2}+\Pi^{\gamma}_{L}}B_{\mu\nu}\right]\,.

(5.24)

Now we can find the dispersion relations using the HTL effective propagator in Eq. (
5.24
) in the thermal medium. The poles of the propagator give the dispersion relations:

\displaystyle P^{2}+\Pi^{\gamma}_{L}

\displaystyle=0\,\implies\omega^{2}-p^{2}+\frac{P^{2}(m^{\gamma}_{D})^{2}}{p^{2}}\left[1-\frac{\omega}{2p}\ln{\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}}\right]=0\,,

(5.25a)

\displaystyle P^{2}+\Pi^{\gamma}_{T}

\displaystyle=0\,\implies\omega^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{(m^{\gamma}_{D})^{2}}{2}\frac{\omega^{2}}{p^{2}}\left[1+\frac{p^{2}-\omega^{2}}{2\omega p}\ln{\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}}\right]=0\,.

(5.25b)

The first condition in Eq. (
5.25a
) gives the longitudinal mode of propagation
\omega_{L}
known as plasmon and the second condition in Eq. (
5.25b
) gives the transverse mode
\omega_{T}
, which is doubly degenerate. The plasmon mode arises due to the presence of the thermal medium. The dispersion relations for photon at one loop are plotted in Fig.
5.4
. Energy of photon splits into two modes due to the breaking of Lorentz symmetry in presence of thermal medium. The longitudenal one is a long wavelength mode which reduces to free dispersion very fast than the transverse one.

Figure 5.4
:
Dispersion of photon in HTL approximation in thermal medium.

It is possible to find out the approximate solutions of
\omega_{T\atop L}
for small and large values of momentum. For small momentum,
p\ll m_{D}^{\gamma}
, one finds

\displaystyle\omega_{T}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{m_{D}^{\gamma}}{\sqrt{3}}\left[1+\frac{9}{5}\frac{p^{2}}{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}}-\frac{81}{35}\frac{p^{4}}{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{4}}+\frac{792}{125}\frac{p^{6}}{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{6}}\right]\,,

(5.26a)

\displaystyle\omega_{L}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{m_{D}^{\gamma}}{\sqrt{3}}\left[1+\frac{9}{10}\frac{p^{2}}{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}}-\frac{27}{280}\frac{p^{4}}{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{4}}+\frac{9}{2000}\frac{p^{6}}{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{6}}\right]\,.

(5.26b)

For momentum,
p\gg m_{D}^{\gamma}
, one finds

\displaystyle\omega_{T}

\displaystyle\approx p+\frac{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}}{2p}+\frac{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{4}}{32p^{3}}\left[3-2\ln\frac{8p^{2}}{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle+\ \frac{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{6}}{128p^{5}}\left[2\ln^{2}\frac{8p^{2}}{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}}-10\ln\frac{8p^{2}}{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}}+7\right]\,,

(5.27a)

\displaystyle\omega_{L}

\displaystyle\approx p+2p\exp\left[-\frac{2(p^{2}+(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2})}{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}}\right]\,.

(5.27b)

5.7  Spectral Representation of Gauge Boson Propagator

The effective propagator of a photon in presence of thermal medium is given in (
5.24
) as discussed in subsec.
5.6
that
P^{2}+\Pi_{L}=0
has solutions at
\omega=\pm\omega_{L}
and
P^{2}+\Pi_{T}=0
has solutions at
\omega=\pm\omega_{T}
. Both also have a cut part due to space like momentum
\omega^{2}<p^{2}
. In-medium spectral function corresponding to the effective photon propagator in (
5.24
) will have both pole and cut contribution as

\displaystyle\rho_{L}(\omega,p)=\rho_{L}^{\textrm{pole}}(\omega,p)+\rho_{L}^{\textrm{cut}}(\omega,p)\,,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho_{T}(\omega,p)=\rho_{T}^{\textrm{pole}}(\omega,p)+\rho_{T}^{\textrm{cut}}(\omega,p)\,.

(5.28)

The pole part of the longitudinal spectral function can be obtained using (
A.5
) as

\displaystyle\rho^{\textrm{pole}}_{L}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{\pi}{\textrm{Im}}\left.\frac{1}{P^{2}+\Pi_{L}}\right|_{\omega\rightarrow\omega+i\epsilon}

(5.29)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\omega}{p^{2}+(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}-\omega^{2}}\left[\delta(\omega-\omega_{L})+\delta(\omega+\omega_{L})\right].

Similarly, the pole part corresponding to the transverse spectral function can be obtained using (
A.5
) as

\displaystyle\rho^{\textrm{pole}}_{T}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{\pi}{\textrm{Im}}\left.\frac{1}{P^{2}+\Pi_{T}}\right|_{\omega\rightarrow\omega+i\epsilon}

(5.30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\omega(\omega^{2}-p^{2})}{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}\omega^{2}+p^{2}(\omega^{2}-p^{2})}\left[\delta(\omega-\omega_{T})+\delta(\omega+\omega_{T})\right].

For
\omega^{2}<p^{2}
, there is a discontinuity in
\ln\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}
as
\ln{y}=\ln\left|y\right|-i\pi\ ,
which leads to the spectral function,
\rho^{\textrm{cut}}_{L,T}(\omega,p)
, corresponding to the discontinuity in
P^{2}+\Pi_{L,T}
. The cut contribution to the longitudinal spectral function can be obtained using Eq. (
A.3
) as

\displaystyle\rho^{\textrm{cut}}_{L}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle\frac{(1-x^{2})x\,m_{D}^{2}\,\Theta(1-x^{2})/2}{\left[p^{2}(1-x^{2})+(1-x^{2})(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}-(1-x^{2})x\,\frac{(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}}{2}\ln\left|\frac{x+1}{x-1}\right|\right]^{2}+\left[\frac{1}{2}\pi(1-x^{2})x\,(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}\right]^{2}}

(5.31)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{L}(x)\Theta(1-x^{2})\,,

where
x=\omega/p
. Similarly, the cut part of the transverse spectral function can be obtained using (
A.3
) as

\displaystyle\rho^{\textrm{cut}}_{T}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{(1-x^{2})x\,(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}\,\Theta(1-x^{2})/4}{\left[p^{2}(1-x^{2})+\frac{1}{2}(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}\left(x^{2}+\frac{(1-x^{2})x}{2}\,\ln\left|\frac{x+1}{x-1}\right|\right)\right]^{2}+\left[\frac{1}{4}\pi(1-x^{2})x\,(m_{D}^{\gamma})^{2}\right]^{2}}

(5.32)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{T}(x)\Theta(1-x^{2})\,.

5.8  Electron-Photon Vertex in HTL Approximation

5.8.1  Three-point function: electron-photon vertex in HTL approximation

Figure 5.5
:
One-loop HTL effective three-point QED vertex

The one-loop correction to the three-point electron-photon vertex is shown in Fig.
5.5
. Using Feyman rules defined in subsec
5.1.1
in Feynman gauge, the one-loop expression for the three-point vertex in Minkowski space becomes

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma_{\mu}(P_{1},P_{2};P_{1}-P_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e^{2}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\left[\gamma_{\alpha}\left(\not{K}-\not{P}_{2}\right)\gamma_{\mu}\left(\not{K}-\not{P}_{1}\right)\gamma^{\alpha}\right]\Delta_{B}(K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{2}-K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{1}-K)\,.

(5.33)

In HTL approxomation, the external momenta in the numerator can be neglected and (
5.33
) is simplified as

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma_{\mu}\left(P_{1},P_{2};P_{1}-P_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{2}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\left[4\not{K}{K}_{\mu}-2K^{2}\gamma_{\mu}\right]\Delta_{B}(K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{2}-K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{1}-K),

(5.34)

where we have used the relations in Minkowski space

\not{K}\gamma_{\mu}\not{K}=2\not{K}K_{\mu}-K^{2}\gamma_{\mu}
and
\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{\alpha}=-2\gamma_{\mu}
.
Now using Saclay method for frequency sum derived in (
2.17
) and using the following simplifications within approximation
[
11
,
62
,
63
]
for massless case (
k=E_{k}
and
q=|\bm{\vec{p}}-\bm{\vec{k}}|
)

\displaystyle q\,=\,\left|\bm{\vec{p}}-\bm{\vec{k}}\right|

\displaystyle\,=\,\sqrt{p^{2}+k^{2}-2pk\cos\theta}=\sqrt{p^{2}+k^{2}-2pkc}\approx k-pc=k-{\bm{\vec{p}\cdot\hat{k}}}\,,

(5.35a)

\displaystyle n_{F}(q)

\displaystyle\,=n_{F}(k-{\bm{\vec{p}\cdot\hat{k}}})\approx n_{F}(k)-{\bm{\vec{p}\cdot\hat{k}}}\frac{dn_{F}(k)}{dk}\,,

(5.35b)

\displaystyle\omega\pm k\pm q

\displaystyle\,=\omega\pm k\pm k\mp\,{\bm{\vec{p}\cdot\hat{k}}}\approx\pm 2k\,,

(5.35c)

\displaystyle\omega\pm k\mp q

\displaystyle\,=\omega\pm k\mp k\pm\,{\bm{\vec{p}\cdot\hat{k}}}\approx\omega\pm{\bm{\vec{p}\cdot\hat{k}}}\,,

(5.35d)

Using the approximations in Eq. (
5.35a
), (
5.35b
), (
5.35c
) and, (
5.35d
),the second term in Eq. (
5.34
) becomes proportional to
\ln T
, which is subleading in
T
and can be neglected. So, the leading order term in Eq. (
5.34
) becomes

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma_{\mu}\left(P_{1},P_{2};P_{1}-P_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4e^{2}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\not{K}{K}_{\mu}\Delta_{B}(K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{2}-K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{1}-K).

(5.36)

The spatial and temporal parts of the three-point function can be written as

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma_{i}\left(P_{1},P_{2};P_{1}-P_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4e^{2}T\sum_{k_{0}=2n\pi iT}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[\gamma_{0}k_{0}-\left(\bm{\vec{\gamma}}\cdot\bm{\vec{k}}\right)\right]k_{i}\Delta_{B}(K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{2}-K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{1}-K).

(5.37)

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma_{0}\left(P_{1},P_{2};P_{1}-P_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4e^{2}T\sum_{k_{0}=2n\pi iT}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[\gamma_{0}k_{0}^{2}-\left(\bm{\vec{\gamma}}\cdot\bm{\vec{k}}\right)k_{0}\right]\Delta_{B}(K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{2}-K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{1}-K).

(5.38)

Now we need to perform following three types of frequency sum:

\displaystyle S_{1}=T\sum_{k_{0}=2n\pi iT}\Delta_{B}(K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{2}-K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{1}-K),

(5.39a)

\displaystyle S_{2}=T\sum_{k_{0}=2n\pi iT}k_{0}\Delta_{B}(K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{2}-K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{1}-K),

(5.39b)

\displaystyle S_{3}=T\sum_{k_{0}=2n\pi iT}k^{2}_{0}\Delta_{B}(K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{2}-K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{1}-K).

(5.39c)

For massless particles
E=k
,
E_{1}=q_{1}=|\bm{\vec{p}_{1}}-\bm{\vec{k}}|
and
E_{2}=q_{2}=|\bm{\vec{p}_{2}}-\bm{\vec{k}}|
.
the frequency sum can be performed using the results given in Eq. (
2.24
). Then performing the sum over
s,s_{1},s_{2}=\pm 1
, one will get eight terms but all of them would not contribute to
T^{2}
order.
It turns out that the
T^{2}
behaviour comes from
S_{1}
if one performs the sum over
s
,
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
in (
5.39a
) with the condition
s=-s_{1}=-s_{2}
and Eq. (
5.39a
) becomes

\displaystyle S_{1}=\frac{1}{8kq_{1}q_{2}}\left\{\frac{1}{D_{1}}\left[\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{+}(q_{1})}{p_{10}-k+q_{1}}-\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{+}(q_{2})}{p_{20}-k+q_{2}}\right]+\frac{1}{D_{2}}\left[\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{-}(q_{1})}{p_{10}+k-q_{1}}-\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{-}(q_{2})}{p_{20}+k-q_{2}}\right]\right\}\,,

(5.40)

where
D_{1}=(p_{10}-p_{20}+q_{1}-q_{2})
and
D_{2}=(p_{10}-p_{20}-q_{1}+q_{2})
. Now, again using HTL approximation in (
5.35a
) to (
5.35d
), one can simplify (
5.40
) as

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8k^{3}}\left[\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{+}(k)}{(P_{1}\cdot\hat{K})(P_{2}\cdot\hat{K})}+\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{-}(k)}{(P_{1}\cdot\hat{K}^{\prime})(P_{2}\cdot\hat{K}^{\prime})}\right]\,,

(5.41)

where
\hat{K}_{\mu}\equiv(1,\hat{k})
and
\hat{K}^{\prime}_{\mu}\equiv(1,-\hat{k})
. The second frequency sum in Eq. (
5.39b
) can be performed using the result in Eq. (
2.26
) and then performing the sum over
s
,
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
with the restriction

s=-s_{1}=-s_{2}
and using HTL approximation, one gets

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8k^{2}}\left[\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{+}(k)}{(P_{1}\cdot\hat{K})(P_{2}\cdot\hat{K})}-\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{-}(k)}{(P_{1}\cdot\hat{K}^{\prime})(P_{2}\cdot\hat{K}^{\prime})}\right]\,.

(5.42)

The third frequency sum in (
5.39c
) can be performed using the result in Eq. (
2.27
), then performing the sum over
s
,
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
with the restriction
s=-s_{1}=-s_{2}
and using HTL approximation, one gets

\displaystyle S_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8k}\left[\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{+}(k)}{(P_{1}\cdot\hat{K})(P_{2}\cdot\hat{K})}+\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{-}(k)}{(P_{1}\cdot\hat{K}^{\prime})(P_{2}\cdot\hat{K}^{\prime})}\right]=k^{2}S_{1}\,.

(5.43)

The spatial part of the three-point function in Eq. (
5.37
) can be written using Eqs. (
5.41
) and (
5.42
) as

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!dk\,k_{i}[2n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{+}(k)+n_{F}^{-}(k)]\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\frac{\gamma_{0}-\bm{\vec{\gamma}}\cdot\bm{\hat{k}}}{(P_{1}\cdot\hat{K})(P_{2}\cdot\hat{K})}

(5.44)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}\right)^{2}\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\frac{{\hat{k}_{i}}\hat{\not{K}}}{(P_{1}\cdot\hat{K})(P_{2}\cdot\hat{K})}.

The temporal part of the three-point function in Eq. (
5.38
) can be written using Eq. (
5.42
) and (
5.43
) as

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dk\,k[2n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{+}(k)+n_{F}^{-}(k)]\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\frac{\gamma_{0}-\bm{\vec{\gamma}}\cdot\bm{\hat{k}}}{(P_{1}\cdot\hat{K})(P_{2}\cdot\hat{K})}

(5.45)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}\right)^{2}\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\frac{\hat{\not{K}}}{(P_{1}\cdot\hat{K})(P_{2}\cdot\hat{K})}.

Using Eqs. (
5.44
) and (
5.45
) one can write the three-point function in compact notation as

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma_{\mu}\left(P_{1},P_{2};P_{1}-P_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}\right)^{2}\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\frac{{\hat{K}_{\mu}}\hat{\not{K}}}{(P_{1}\cdot\hat{K})(P_{2}\cdot\hat{K})}=-\left(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}\right)^{2}\left\langle\frac{{\hat{K}_{\mu}}\hat{\not{K}}}{(P_{1}\cdot\hat{K})(P_{2}\cdot\hat{K})}\right\rangle_{\bm{\hat{k}}}\,,

(5.46)

We now check the Ward-Takahashi identity for QED as

\displaystyle\left(P_{1}-P_{2}\right)_{\mu}\delta\Gamma^{\mu}\left(P_{1},P_{2};P_{1}-P_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(P_{1}-P_{2}\right)_{\mu}\left[-\left(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}\right)^{2}\left\langle\frac{{\hat{K}^{\mu}}\hat{\not{K}}}{(P_{1}\cdot\hat{K})(P_{2}\cdot\hat{K})}\right\rangle_{\bm{\hat{k}}}\right]=\Sigma^{\textrm{e}}(P_{1})-\Sigma^{\textrm{e}}(P_{2})\,,

(5.47)

where the electron self-energy
\Sigma^{\textrm{e}}(P)
is obtained in (
5.8
). The 3-point function in QED is

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}(P_{1},P_{2};P_{1}-P_{2})=\gamma_{\mu}-\delta\Gamma_{\mu}=\gamma_{\mu}+\left(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}\right)^{2}\left\langle\frac{{\hat{K}_{\mu}}\hat{\not{K}}}{(P_{1}\cdot\hat{K})(P_{2}\cdot\hat{K})}\right\rangle_{\bm{\hat{k}}}\,,

(5.48)

5.8.2  Four-point function: two photon-two electron vertex in HTL approximation

Figure 5.6
:
One-loop HTL correction to the four-point QED vertex

Following Feynman rules in subsec
5.1.1
, the 4-point function in Fig.
5.6
(a)
can be obtained as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{(a)}\left(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e^{2}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\left[\gamma_{\alpha}\left(\not{K}-\not{P}_{2}\right)\gamma_{\mu}\left(\not{K}-\not{P}_{1}-\not{Q}_{1}\right)\gamma_{\nu}\left(\not{K}-\not{P}_{1}\right)\gamma^{\alpha}\right]

(5.49)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\ \times\ \Delta_{B}(K)\Delta_{F}(P_{2}-K)\Delta_{F}(P_{1}-K+Q_{1})\Delta_{F}(P_{1}-K).

In HTL approximation, the Eq. (
5.49
) can be simplified as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{(a)}\left(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e^{2}T\sum_{k_{0}}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}(2\gamma^{\mu}K^{\nu}+\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\not{K})\Delta_{F}(P_{2}-K)\Delta_{F}(P_{1}-K+Q_{1})\Delta_{F}(K-P_{1})

(5.50)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 8e^{2}T\sum_{k_{0}}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}K^{\mu}K^{\nu}\not{K}\Delta_{B}(K)\Delta_{F}(K-P_{2})\Delta_{F}(P_{1}-K+Q_{1})\Delta_{F}(K-P_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{(1a)}\left(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1}\right)+\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{(2a)}\left(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1}\right)\,,

where
k_{0}=2n\pi iT
. The above Eq. (
5.50
) involves 3 and 4 propagators.
Now, using (
2.29
) with

E=r=|\bm{\vec{k}}-\bm{\vec{p}_{1}}|=k-\bm{\hat{k}\cdot\vec{p}_{1}}
,
E_{1}=r_{1}=|\bm{\vec{p}_{2}-\vec{k}}|=k-\bm{\hat{k}\cdot\vec{p}_{2}}
, and

E_{2}=r_{2}=|\bm{\vec{p}_{1}}-\bm{\vec{k}}+\bm{\vec{q_{1}}}|=k-\bm{\hat{k}\cdot(\vec{p}_{1}+\vec{q}_{1})}
for massless particles, the frequency sum involving
3 propagators,
\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{(1a)}\left(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1}\right)
can be performed. Then performing
the sum over
s,s_{1},s_{2}=\pm 1
, one will get eight terms but all of which would not contribute. As we have seen for 3-point function earlier, here also
six terms under HTL approximation contribute to the non-leading order in
T
.
Now, under HTL approximation the momentum integration of the remaining two terms
of
T\sum_{k_{0}}\int_{\bm{k}}k_{i}\Delta_{F}(K-P_{1})\Delta_{F}(P_{2}-K)\Delta_{F}(P_{1}-K+Q_{1})
yields the following result:

\displaystyle\int\frac{1}{8k^{3}}k_{i}\ d^{3}k\frac{dn_{F}}{dk}\sim\int\ dk\frac{dn_{F}}{dk}\sim T^{0},

(5.51)

which can be safely neglected.
So, the first term,
\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{(1a)}(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1})
, in (
5.50
) does not contribute in HTL approximation.
Now, we will calculate the second term
\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{(2a)}\left(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1}\right)
of equation (
5.50
).
To calculate the frequency sum of
\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{(2a)}\left(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1}\right)
, we can define the following tensor

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{a}_{\mu\nu\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T\sum_{k_{0}}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}}K_{\mu}K_{\nu}K_{\beta}\Delta_{B}(K){\Delta_{F}}(P_{1}-K){\Delta_{F}}(P_{2}-K){\Delta_{F}}(P_{1}-K+Q_{1}),

(5.52)

whose components are needed to be computed:

\displaystyle X_{0}^{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{0}=2\pi inT}\Delta_{B}(K)\Delta_{F}(P_{1}-K)\Delta_{F}(P_{2}-K)\Delta_{F}(P_{1}-K+Q_{1})\,,

(5.53a)

\displaystyle X_{1}^{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{0}=2\pi inT}k_{0}\Delta_{B}(K)\Delta_{F}(P_{1}-K)\Delta_{F}(P_{2}-K)\Delta_{F}(P_{1}-K+Q_{1})\,,

(5.53b)

\displaystyle X_{2}^{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{0}=2\pi inT}k_{0}^{2}\Delta_{B}(K)\Delta_{F}(P_{1}-K)\Delta_{F}(P_{2}-K)\Delta_{F}(P_{1}-K+Q_{1})=k^{2}X_{0}^{a}\,,

(5.53c)

\displaystyle X_{3}^{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{0}=2\pi inT}k_{0}^{3}\Delta_{B}(K)\Delta_{F}(P_{1}-K)\Delta_{F}(P_{2}-K)\Delta_{F}(P_{1}-K+Q_{1})=k^{2}X_{1}^{a}\,.

(5.53d)

The Matsubara sums needed to evaluate the above equations are given in (
2.31
) and (
2.32
). Using (
2.31
) with
E=k;\ E_{1}=k-\bm{\vec{p}_{1}\cdot\hat{k}};\ E_{2}=k-\bm{\vec{p}_{2}\cdot\hat{k}};\ E_{3}=k-\bm{(\vec{p}_{1}+\vec{q}_{1})\cdot\hat{k}}
for massless particles, then performing sum over
-s=s_{1}=s_{2}=s_{3}
and keeping only
T^{2}
order terms within HTL approximation, we get

\displaystyle X^{a}_{0}=-\frac{1}{16k^{4}}\left[\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{+}(k)}{P_{1}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ P_{2}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ (P_{1}+Q_{1})\!\cdot\!\hat{K}}-\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{-}(k)}{P_{1}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}^{\prime}\ P_{2}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}^{\prime}\ (P_{1}+Q_{1})\!\cdot\!\hat{K}^{\prime}}\right].

(5.54)

The remaining frequency sums in (
5.53b
) to (
5.53d
) can be obtained as

\displaystyle X^{a}_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8k^{3}}\left[\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{+}(k)}{P_{1}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ P_{2}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ (P_{1}+Q_{1})\!\cdot\!\hat{K}}+\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{-}(k)}{P_{1}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}^{\prime}\ P_{2}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}^{\prime}\ (P_{1}+Q_{1})\!\cdot\!\hat{K}^{\prime}}\right]\,,

(5.55a)

\displaystyle X^{a}_{2}

\displaystyle=k^{2}X^{a}_{0}=-\frac{1}{8k^{2}}\left[\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{+}(k)}{P_{1}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ P_{2}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ (P_{1}+Q_{1})\!\cdot\!\hat{K}}-\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{-}(k)}{P_{1}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}^{\prime}\ P_{2}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}^{\prime}\ (P_{1}+Q_{1})\!\cdot\!\hat{K}^{\prime}}\right]\,,

(5.55b)

\displaystyle X^{a}_{3}

\displaystyle=k^{2}X^{a}_{0}=-\frac{1}{8k}\left[\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{+}(k)}{P_{1}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ P_{2}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ (P_{1}+Q_{1})\!\cdot\!\hat{K}}+\frac{n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{-}(k)}{P_{1}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}^{\prime}\ P_{2}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}^{\prime}\ (P_{1}+Q_{1})\cdot\hat{K}^{\prime}}\right]\,.

(5.55c)

Using the results of the frequency sums from equations (
5.54
) to (
5.55c
), then tensor in (
5.52
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{a}_{\mu\nu\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int\frac{k\ dk}{32\pi^{2}}\left[2n_{B}(k)+n_{F}^{+}(k)+n_{F}^{-}(k)\right]\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\ \frac{\hat{K}_{\mu}\hat{K}_{\nu}\hat{K}_{\beta}}{P_{1}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ P_{2}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ (P_{1}+Q_{1})\!\cdot\!\hat{K}}

(5.56)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{T^{2}}{64}\left(T^{2}+\frac{\mu^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\right)\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\ \frac{\hat{K}_{\mu}\hat{K}_{\nu}\hat{K}_{\beta}}{P_{1}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ P_{2}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ (P_{1}+Q_{1})\!\cdot\!\hat{K}}.

So, the first diagram of the four point vertex (leading order) in Fig.
5.6
(a)
given in (
5.50
) becomes

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{(a)}\left(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(m_{\rm{th}}^{e})^{2}\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\ \frac{\hat{K}_{\mu}\hat{K}_{\nu}\hat{\not{K}}}{P_{1}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ P_{2}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ (P_{1}+Q_{1})\!\cdot\!\hat{K}}.

(5.57)

The second diagram of Fig.
5.6
(b)
can be calculated in the similar manner and it becomes

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{(b)}\left(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(m_{\rm{th}}^{e})^{2}\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\ \frac{\hat{K}_{\mu}\hat{K}_{\nu}\not{K}}{P_{1}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ P_{2}\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ (P_{2}-Q_{1})\!\cdot\!\hat{K}}.

(5.58)

So, the QED four-point vertex within te HTL approximation from the two diagrams of Fig.
5.6
becomes

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu\nu}\left(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{(a)}\left(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1}\right)+\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{(b)}\left(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1}\right)

(5.59)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(m_{\rm{th}}^{e})^{2}\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\ \frac{\hat{K}_{\mu}\hat{K}_{\nu}\hat{\not{K}}}{\left(P_{1}+Q_{1}\right)\!\cdot\!\hat{K}\ \left(P_{2}-Q_{1}\right)\!\cdot\!\hat{K}}\left[\frac{1}{P_{1}\cdot\hat{K}}+\frac{1}{P_{2}\cdot\hat{K}}\right].

The QED four-point function in Eq. (
5.59
) satisfies the Ward identity, namely,

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{\mu}\Gamma_{\mu\nu}\left(P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1}\right)=\Gamma_{\nu}\left(P_{1},P_{2}-Q_{1}\right)-\Gamma_{\nu}\left(P_{1}+Q_{1},P_{2}\right).

(5.60)

Additionally,
\Gamma_{\mu\mu}\simeq 0

Lastly, we now note that since the photons do not self-interact the three and four photon vertex do not exist. So the non-zero
N
-point HTL functions in QED are photon self-energy (
\Pi^{\gamma}_{\mu\nu}
), the electron self-energy (
\Sigma^{\rm e}
) and
N
-photon- two electron vertex.

5.9  HTL Improved QED Lagrangian

Following the results obtained in previous subsections
5.2
,
5.5
,
5.8.1
and
5.8.2
, the HTL contribution to the QED
Lagrangian
[
12
,
14
,
15
]
can be written as

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny QED}}^{\textrm{\tiny HTL}}={\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny QED}}+{\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny HTL}}\,

(5.61)

where
{\cal L}_{\textrm{QED}}
is usual QED lagrangian in vacuum as given in (
5.1
). The HTL contribution to effective QED Lagrangian can be written
in compact form as

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny HTL}}=i\left(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}\right)^{2}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\left\langle\frac{\hat{K}_{\mu}}{\hat{K}\!\cdot\!D}\right\rangle_{\bm{\hat{k}}}\psi-\frac{1}{2}\left(m^{\gamma}_{D}\right)^{2}\left(F_{\mu\alpha}\left\langle\frac{\hat{K}^{\alpha}\hat{K}_{\beta}}{(\hat{K}\!\cdot\!D)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\bm{\hat{k}}}F^{\mu\beta}\right)\,,

(5.62)

where
F
is the electromagnetic field strength,
D
is the covariant derivative. The two parameters
m^{\gamma}_{D}
and
m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}
are, respectively, the Debye screening mass of photon and the thermal electron mass which take into account the
screening effects. These are, respectively, given in (
5.18
) and (
5.7
). The Lagrangian is non-local and gauge symmetric, which forces the presence of the covariant derivative
in the denominator of (
5.62
) and makes it also non-linear. It is also clear from the first term in (
5.62
) there are no HTL with more than two electron lines.
When expanded in powers of electron and photon fields,(
5.62
) generates an infinite series of non-local self-energy and vertex corrections (viz., HTL
N
-point QED
functions obtained in subsections
5.2
,
5.5
,
5.8.1
and
5.8.2
) to the bare one. These
N
-point functions are related by Ward-Takahashi identity. If it is
expanded in terms of electron fields, (
5.62
) will lead to electron self-energy as obtained in (
5.8
) whereas when expanded in two fermions and leading order in
gauge field, one gets back
3
-point electron-photon vertex as obtained in (
5.46
) and two electrons and quadratic order in gauge lead to
4
-point function (two electron-two
photon vertex) as obtained in (
5.59
). It is evident that the second term in (
5.62
) is bilinear in photon field, HTL Green’s function with
N
external photons (
N\geq 3
) and no external electron lines are zero
[
48
]
. Very recently, the complete correction to the HTL Lagrangian up to NLO for electron-positron plasma is calculated in Ref.
[
77
]
.

6  Quantum Chromodynamics (QCD)

QCD is the theory of strong interaction which is a non-abelian
SU(3)
gauge theory. It describes the quarks and gluons in the similar way as QED does for electrons and photons. The major difference between the two theories is that QCD contains three colour charges in fundamental representation. Consequently, a quark can be represented by a vector with three colour states. The interaction among colour charges is facilitated by gauge fields, notably the gluon. The special unitary group
SU(3)
has
3^{2}-1=8
generators, the number of charge mediating particles, corresponding to eight gluons in QCD. Unlike QED in photon, the gluons in QCD exhibit self-interaction.

6.1  Lagrangian

The QCD Lagrangian is written as

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny QCD}}={\bar{\psi}}_{if}(i\not{D}-m_{q})\psi_{if}-\frac{1}{4}G_{\mu\nu}^{a}G^{\mu\nu}_{a}+{\cal L}_{\textrm{gf}}+{\cal L}_{\textrm{gh}}+\delta{\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny QCD}}\,.

(6.1)

We note that
\psi_{if}
is quark spinor with colour indices
i=(\textrm{r, g, b})
and
f
indicates the flavour index and
m_{q}
is the current quark mass.
\delta{\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny QCD}}
is the renormalised counter terms. The Lagrangian in Eq. (
6.1
) is quite similar to QED Lagrangian in Eq. (
5.1
), with the differences that the electromagnetic field strength tensor
F^{\mu\nu}
, is replaced by the gluonic field strength tensor
G^{a}_{\mu\nu}
and an another set of indices corresponding to colour degrees of freedom. The gauge covariant derivative is

\displaystyle D_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}+igt_{a}A^{a}_{\mu}\,,

(6.2)

where
g
is the strong coupling constant,
t_{a}
are the generators of
SU(3)
gauge group and
A_{\mu}^{a}
is gluon a field. The
t_{a}
is defined as
t_{a}=\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{a}
, where
\lambda_{a}
are the Gell-Mann matrices which were not there in QED.
\lambda_{a}
matrices change the colour of the interacting particles. These matrices are traceless and obey the commutation relation
[\lambda_{a},\,\lambda_{b}]=if_{abc}\lambda^{c}
with normalisation relation
{\textrm{Tr}}(\lambda_{a}\lambda_{b})=2\delta_{ab}
,
f^{abc}
is the structure constant of the group which is number. It is also totally antisymmetric and vanishes if two of the indices become same. Now,
G^{a}_{\mu\nu}
indicates the gluon field tensor which is invariant under gauge transformation and can be defined as

G_{a}^{\mu\nu}=F_{a}^{\mu\nu}-gf_{abc}A_{b}^{\mu}A_{c}^{\nu}\,,

where the first term is similar to the QED field tensor but with colour index and the second term represents the self interaction of gluons. In contrast to QED, the mediator gluons have colour charge which enable them to interact among themselves. Now the gluonic part of the Lagrangian can be written as

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny G}}=-\frac{1}{4}G_{a\mu\nu}{G^{\mu\nu}_{a}}=-\frac{1}{4}F_{a\mu\nu}{F^{\mu\nu}_{a}}+gf_{abc}A_{a\mu}A_{b\nu}\partial^{\mu}A_{c}^{\nu}-\frac{1}{4}g^{2}f_{abc}f_{alm}A^{\mu}_{b}A^{\nu}_{c}A_{l\mu}A_{m\nu}\,.

(6.3)

Finally, the gauge fixing and ghost terms in covariant gauge are, respectively, given as

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\textrm{gf}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\xi}(\partial_{\mu}A_{a}^{\mu})^{2}\,,

(6.4a)

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\textrm{gh}}

\displaystyle=-{\bar{C}}_{a}\partial^{2}C_{a}-gf_{abc}{\bar{C}}_{a}\partial_{\mu}(A^{\mu}_{b}C_{c})\,,

(6.4b)

where
\xi
is the gauge fixing parameter and
C
is the ghost field which is a Grassmann variable. The gauge fixing
Lagrangian in Eq. (
6.4a
) is required to eliminate the unphysical degrees of freedom present in the theory. The ghost Lagrangian in covariant gauge is given in (
6.4b
) which depends on the choice of the gauge fixing term.

Combining Eqs. (
6.2
), (
6.3
), (
6.4a
) and (
6.4b
), the QCD Lagrangian in Eq. (
6.1
) becomes

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny QCD}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}F_{a\mu\nu}{F^{\mu\nu}_{a}}-\frac{1}{2\xi}(\partial_{\mu}A_{a}^{\mu})^{2}+{\bar{\psi}}_{if}(i\not{\partial}-m_{q})\psi_{if}-g({\bar{\psi}}_{if}t_{a}\psi_{if})\not{A}^{a}+gf_{abc}A_{a\mu}A_{b\nu}\partial^{\mu}A_{c}^{\nu}

(6.5)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}g^{2}f_{abc}f_{alm}A^{\mu}_{b}A^{\nu}_{c}A_{l\mu}A_{m\nu}-{\bar{C}}_{a}\partial^{2}C_{a}-gf_{abc}{\bar{C}}_{a}\partial_{\mu}(A^{\mu}_{b}C_{c})+\delta{\cal L}\,\,.

6.1.1  Feynman rules

We note the following from Eq. (
6.5
):

\bullet

The first term describes the Lagrangian for eight non-interacting, massless spin
1
gluon fields.
The second one corresponds to the gauge fixing term. These two terms together lead to a propagator for free gluon as

\displaystyle\hskip 144.54pt\Rightarrow\,\,\,\,\,\,i(D_{0}(P))^{ab}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{i\delta^{ab}}{P^{2}}\left[-\eta_{\mu\nu}+\left(1-\xi\right)\frac{P_{\mu}P_{\nu}}{P^{2}}\right]=i\delta^{ab}(D_{0}(P))_{\mu\nu}.

(6.6)

\bullet

The third term is the Lagrangian density that describes the free propagation of a non-interacting quark with current mass
m_{q}
. The free quark propagator reads as

\displaystyle\hskip 173.44756pt\Rightarrow\,\,\,\,\,i(S_{0}(P))^{i}_{j}=\frac{i\delta^{i}_{j}}{P\!\!\!\!/\penalty-m_{q}}=i\delta^{i}_{j}S_{0}(P)\,.

(6.7)

\bullet

The fourth term originates from the local gauge symmetry and corresponds to the Lagrangian density of quark and gluon fields interaction through the dimensionless running coupling
g
. The quark-gluon
3
vertex is represented as

\displaystyle\hskip 43.36243pt\Rightarrow\,\,\,-ig\gamma^{\mu}(t^{a})^{i}_{j}.

(6.8)

\bullet

The fifth term corresponds to the Lagrangian density of three gluons self-interaction which has no analogue in QED, as photons do not self-interact. This produces three-gluon vertex in perturbation theory as

\displaystyle\hskip 50.58878pt\Rightarrow\,\,\,-gf^{abc}\Big[\eta^{\alpha\beta}\left(P_{1}-P_{2}\right)^{\gamma}+\eta^{\beta\gamma}\left(P_{2}-P_{3}\right)^{\alpha}+\eta^{\gamma\alpha}\left(P_{3}-P_{1}\right)^{\beta}\Big].

(6.9)

\bullet

The sixth term corresponds to the Lagrangian density of four gluons self-interaction which has also no analogue in QED, as photons do not self-interact. This produces four-gluon vertex in perturbation theory as

\displaystyle\hskip 50.58878pt\Rightarrow\,\,\,-ig^{2}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\,\,\,\,f^{abe}f^{cde}\left(\eta^{\alpha\gamma}\eta^{\beta\delta}-\eta^{\alpha\delta}\eta^{\beta\gamma}\right)\\
+f^{ace}f^{bde}\left(\eta^{\alpha\beta}\eta^{\gamma\delta}-\eta^{\alpha\delta}\eta^{\gamma\beta}\right)\\
+f^{ade}f^{bce}\left(\eta^{\alpha\beta}\eta^{\delta\gamma}-\eta^{\alpha\gamma}\eta^{\delta\beta}\right)\end{array}\right].

\bullet

The seventh term corresponds to the Lagrangian density of ghost field and the propagator is given as

\displaystyle\hskip-36.135pt\Rightarrow\,\,\,\frac{i\delta^{ab}}{P^{2}}\,.

(6.14)

\bullet

The eighth term corresponds to the interaction Lagrangian density between ghost and gluon and the ghost-gluon interaction vertex is given as

\displaystyle\hskip 43.36243pt\Rightarrow\,\,\,-gf^{abc}P^{\prime\mu}.

(6.15)

6.2  Gluon Two-point Functions in HTL Approximation

We begin by noting that the previous discussion on QED in section
5
would offer a good starting point for QCD, given the similarity in their formalism. While QCD is a non-Abelian
SU(3)
gauge theory, QED is a
U(1)
gauge theory. Consequently, extending QED results to QCD primarily involve considering group-theoretical factors, as elaborated below.

6.2.1  Gluon self-energy

We calculate the one-loop gluon self-energy
[
5
,
66
,
67
,
51
]
and the relevant diagrams are given in Fig.
6.1
.

Figure 6.1
:
One-loop gluon self-energy containing four diagrams: (a) corresponds to gluon loop arising from four gluons interaction, (b) represents gluon loop arising from three gluons interaction, (c) is the ghost loop and (d) is the quark loop.

The contribution of the first diagram (tadpole) in Fig.
6.1
(a) in Feynman gauge using the Feynman rules defined in subsec
6.1.1
can be written as

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{({\textrm{a}})}(P)\delta^{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{-i\eta^{\alpha\beta}\delta^{cd}}{K^{2}}(-ig^{2})\Big[f^{cde}f^{bae}\left(\eta_{\alpha\nu}\eta_{\beta\mu}-\eta_{\alpha\mu}\eta_{\beta\nu}\right)

(6.16)

\displaystyle+f^{cbe}f^{dae}\left(\eta_{\alpha\beta}\eta_{\nu\mu}-\eta_{\alpha\mu}\eta_{\nu\beta}\right)+f^{cae}f^{dbe}\left(\eta_{\alpha\beta}\eta_{\mu\nu}-\eta_{\alpha\nu}\eta_{\mu\beta}\right)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-3g^{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\Big[f^{cae}f^{cbe}\Big]\Delta_{B}(K)=-3g^{2}C_{A}\delta^{ab}\eta_{\mu\nu}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\Delta_{B}(K)\,,

where
g
is the strong or QCD coupling constant,
C_{A}\,(N_{c}=3)
is the group factor and
\Delta_{B}(K)=1/K^{2}
is the scalar part of the gluon propagator.

The contribution of the second diagram (gluon loop) in Fig.
6.1
(b) can be written using the Feynman rules defined subsec
6.1.1
as

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{({\textrm{b}})}(P)\delta^{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{-i\eta^{\alpha\beta}\delta^{cd}}{K^{2}}\ \frac{-i\eta^{\sigma\rho}\delta^{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}}{(K-P)^{2}}\times\left(-gf^{acc^{\prime}}\right)\Big[\eta_{\mu\alpha}\left(P+K\right)_{\rho}+\eta_{\alpha\rho}\left(-2K+P\right)_{\mu}+\eta_{\rho\mu}\left(K-2P\right)_{\alpha}\Big]

(6.17)

\displaystyle\times\left(-gf^{bdd^{\prime}}\right)\Big[\eta_{\nu\beta}\left(-P-K\right)_{\sigma}+\eta_{\beta\sigma}\left(2K-P\right)_{\nu}+\eta_{\sigma\nu}\left(-K+2P\right)_{\beta}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}g^{2}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\Delta_{B}(K)\Delta_{B}(P-K)f^{acc^{\prime}}f^{bcc^{\prime}}\,{\cal T}_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{C_{A}}{2}g^{2}\delta^{ab}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\Delta_{B}(K)\Delta_{B}(P-K){\cal T}_{\mu\nu}\,

where
\Delta_{B}(P-k)=1/(P-K)^{2}
, and the tensorial structure
{\cal T}_{\mu\nu}
is given as

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{\mu\nu}\!\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\eta^{\alpha\beta}\eta^{\sigma\rho}\Big[\eta_{\mu\alpha}\left(P+K\right)_{\rho}+\eta_{\alpha\rho}\left(-2K+P\right)_{\mu}+\eta_{\rho\mu}\left(K-2P\right)_{\alpha}\Big]\Big[\eta_{\nu\beta}\left(-P-K\right)_{\sigma}+\eta_{\beta\sigma}\left(2K-P\right)_{\nu}+\eta_{\sigma\nu}\left(-K+2P\right)_{\beta}\Big]

(6.18)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle-2\Big[5K_{\mu}K_{\nu}+\eta_{\mu\nu}K^{2}\Big]\,,

where the soft external momentum (
P\sim gT
) in the numerator, i.e., one or higher power of
P
are neglected. Now combining (
6.18
) with (
6.17
),
one obtains

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{({\textrm{b}})}(P)\delta^{ab}=g^{2}C_{A}\delta^{ab}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}5K_{\mu}K_{\nu}\Delta_{B}(K)\Delta_{B}(P-K)+g^{2}C_{A}\delta^{ab}\eta_{\mu\nu}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}K^{2}\Delta_{B}(K)\Delta_{B}(P-K)\,.

(6.19)

The contribution of the third diagram (ghost loop) in Fig.
6.1
(c) can be obtained using the Feynman rules defined subsec
6.1.1
as

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{({\textrm{c}})}(P)\delta^{ab}\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!-\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\Big(-gf^{ac^{\prime}c}\Big)K_{\mu}\ \frac{i\delta^{cd}}{K^{2}}\Big(-gf^{bdd^{\prime}}\Big)(K-P)_{\nu}\frac{i\delta^{d^{\prime}c^{\prime}}}{(K-P)^{2}}

(6.20)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt=-g^{2}\!\int\!\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}K_{\mu}(K-P)_{\nu}f^{acc^{\prime}}f^{bcc^{\prime}}\Delta_{B}(K)\Delta_{B}(P-K)\approx-g^{2}C_{A}\delta^{ab}\!\int\!\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}K_{\mu}K_{\nu}\Delta_{B}(K)\Delta_{B}(P-K).\,

Combining these three diagrams, one can write

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{{\textrm{(a+b+c)}}}(P)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{2}C_{A}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\left[4K_{\mu}K_{\nu}-2K^{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\right]\Delta_{B}(K)\Delta_{B}(P-K)\,.

(6.21)

Using the result of Matsubara sum listed in sec
2.1.4
and following the procedure explained in Ref.
[
5
]
, we get

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu}^{\mu{\textrm{(a+b+c)}}}(P)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{C_{A}g^{2}T^{2}}{3},\qquad\Pi^{\textrm{(}a+b+c)}_{00}(\omega,p)=\frac{C_{A}g^{2}T^{2}}{3}\left\langle\,\frac{\omega-P\cdot{\hat{K}}}{P\cdot{\hat{K}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{\hat{k}}},

\displaystyle\Pi^{\textrm{(}a+b+c)}_{ij}(p_{0},p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{C_{A}g^{2}T^{2}}{3}\left\langle\,\frac{p_{0}\ {\hat{k}}_{i}{\hat{k}}_{j}}{P\cdot{\hat{K}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{\hat{k}}}\,,\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\Pi^{\textrm{(}a+b+c)}_{0i}(p_{0},p)=\frac{C_{A}g^{2}T^{2}}{3}\left\langle\frac{p_{0}\ {\hat{k}}_{i}}{P\cdot{\hat{K}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{\hat{k}}}

(6.22)

Now the contribution of the fourth diagram (quark loop) in Fig.
6.1
(d) can be obtained using the Feynman rules defined subsec
6.1.1
as

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{({\textrm{d}})}(P)\delta^{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\!\int\!\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}{\rm Tr}\left[\left(-igt^{a}_{ij}\gamma_{\mu}\right)\frac{i}{\not{K}}(-ig\gamma_{\nu}t^{b}_{ji})\frac{i}{\not{K}-\not{P}}\right]

(6.23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-g^{2}N_{f}\!\int\!\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}{\rm Tr}(t^{a}t^{b}){\rm Tr}\left[\frac{\gamma_{\mu}\not{K}\gamma_{\nu}(\not{K}-\not{P})}{K^{2}(P-K)^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle-g^{2}\delta^{ab}\frac{N_{f}}{2}\!\!\int\!\!\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\!\left[8K_{\mu}K_{\nu}-4K^{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\right]\Delta_{F}(K)\Delta_{F}(P-K),\ \

where
N_{f}
is the number of quark flavour and
\Delta_{F}(K)=1/K^{2}
and
\Delta_{F}(P-K)=1/(P-K)^{2}
are the scalar part of the quark propagator.

Note that the fourth diagram (quark loop) in Fig.
6.1
(d) differs from the QED photon self-energy only by a factor
N_{f}/2
only. So, we skip the details calculation here and used the photon self-energy calculation done in sec.
5.5
.

Doing so and adding the contribution of diagram
a,b,c
, the Lorentz contacted gluon self-energy from the four diagrams become

\displaystyle(\Pi^{\textrm{g}})_{\mu}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{g^{2}T^{2}}{3}\left[C_{A}+\frac{N_{f}}{2}\left(1+{3\mu^{2}\over\pi^{2}T^{2}}\right)\right]=-(m^{\textrm{g}}_{D})^{2}\,,

(6.24)

and the QCD Debye mass is given as

\displaystyle(m_{D}^{\textrm{g}})^{2}=\frac{g^{2}T^{2}}{3}\left[C_{A}+\frac{N_{f}}{2}\left(1+{3\mu^{2}\over\pi^{2}T^{2}}\right)\right]\,.

(6.25)

One can also obtain temporal component of self energy from (
5.19
)as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\textrm{g}}_{00}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(m^{\textrm{g}}_{D})^{2}\left\langle\,\frac{\omega-P\cdot{\hat{K}}}{P\cdot{\hat{K}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{\hat{k}}}=(m^{\textrm{g}}_{D})^{2}\left[\frac{\omega}{2p}\ln\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}-1\right]\,,

(6.26)

and spatial components of self-energy from (
5.20
) and (
5.5
), respectively as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\textrm{g}}_{ij}(p_{0},p)=(m^{\textrm{g}}_{D})^{2}\left\langle\frac{p_{0}\ {\hat{k}}_{i}{\hat{k}}_{j}}{P\cdot{\hat{K}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{\hat{k}}}\,,\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\Pi^{\textrm{g}}_{0i}(p_{0},p)=(m^{\textrm{g}}_{D})^{2}\left\langle\frac{p_{0}\ {\hat{k}}_{i}}{P\cdot{\hat{K}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{\hat{k}}}\,.

(6.27a)

Note that, to go from QED to QCD or photon to gluon, one should only change

\displaystyle e^{2}\left(1+{3\mu^{2}\over\pi^{2}T^{2}}\right)\Rightarrow g^{2}\left[C_{A}+\frac{N_{f}}{2}\left(1+{3\mu^{2}\over\pi^{2}T^{2}}\right)\right]\,.

(6.28)

Using (
6.26
) in (
2.75
) one obtains the longitudinal component of the gluon self-energy as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\textrm{g}}_{L}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{P^{2}}{p^{2}}\Pi^{\textrm{g}}_{00}(\omega,p)=\frac{(m^{\textrm{g}}_{D})^{2}P^{2}}{p^{2}}\left[1-\frac{\omega}{2p}\ln\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}\right]\,.

(6.29)

Now using (
6.24
) and (
6.29
) in (
2.76
), one obtains the transverse component of the photon self-energy as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\textrm{g}}_{T}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[(\Pi^{\textrm{g}})_{\mu}^{\mu}-\Pi^{\textrm{g}}_{L}\right]\,=-\frac{(m^{\textrm{g}}_{D})^{2}\omega^{2}}{2p^{2}}\left[1+\frac{p^{2}-\omega^{2}}{2\omega p}\ln\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}\right]\,.

(6.30)

We now note that in the IR limit (
\omega\rightarrow 0
), one gets

\displaystyle\lim_{\omega\rightarrow 0}\Pi^{\textrm{g}}_{L}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle\,=\lim_{\omega\rightarrow 0}-\frac{P^{2}}{p^{2}}\Pi^{\textrm{g}}_{00}(\omega,p)=-(m^{\textrm{g}}_{D})^{2}\,,

(6.31a)

\displaystyle\lim_{\omega\rightarrow 0}\Pi^{\textrm{g}}_{T}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle\,=0\,.

(6.31b)

Equation (
6.31a
) represents the Debye electric screening mass of the gluon, serving as an infrared (IR) regulator at the static electric scale(
\sim gT
). Conversely, Eq. (
6.31b
) indicates the absence of magnetic screening, as the one-loop gluon transverse self-energy in leading order HTL approximation vanishes in the IR limit, providing no magnetic screening mass for gluon.

Figure 6.2
:
The Dyson-Schwinger equation for the HTL effective gluon propagator.

6.2.2  Effective gluon propagator and collective excitations

One obtains the HTL effective gluon propagator using Eq. (
2.79
) and following Dyson-Schwinger equation in Fig.
6.2
in presence of a thermal medium
[
45
,
5
]
as

\displaystyle D^{ab}_{\mu\nu}(P)=-\delta^{ab}\left[\frac{\xi}{P^{4}}P_{\mu}P_{\nu}+\frac{1}{P^{2}+\Pi^{\textrm{g}}_{T}}A_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{P^{2}+\Pi^{\textrm{g}}_{L}}B_{\mu\nu}\right]\,.

(6.32)

Now we can find the gluon dispersion relations using the HTL effective propagator in Eq. (
6.32
) in the thermal medium. The poles of the propagator give the dispersion relations:

\displaystyle P^{2}+\Pi^{\textrm{g}}_{L}

\displaystyle=0\,\,\,\implies\omega^{2}-p^{2}+\frac{P^{2}(m^{\textrm{g}}_{D})^{2}}{p^{2}}\left[1-\frac{\omega}{2p}\ln{\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}}\right]=0\,,

(6.33a)

\displaystyle P^{2}+\Pi^{\textrm{g}}_{T}

\displaystyle=0\,\,\,\implies\omega^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{(m^{\textrm{g}}_{D})^{2}}{2}\frac{\omega^{2}}{p^{2}}\left[1+\frac{p^{2}-\omega^{2}}{2\omega p}\ln{\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}}\right]=0\,.

(6.33b)

Therefore, we note that the collective excitations in a QCD plasma are same as QED plasma excitations as displayed in Fig.
5.4
. One can find out the approximate solutions of
\omega_{T\atop L}
for small and large values of momentum by replacing
m_{D}^{\gamma}
by
m_{D}^{\rm g}
in the respective equation in Eq. (
5.26a
), (
5.26b
), (
5.27a
) and (
5.27b
). One can also obtain the spectral representation of the effective gluon propagators from Eq. (
5.28
) by replacing
m_{D}^{\gamma}
by
m_{D}^{\rm g}
.

6.3  Quark Two-point Functions in HTL Approximation

6.3.1  Quark self-energy

Figure 6.3
:
One-loop quark self-energy diagram.

The evaluation of the quark self-energy at one-loop order is even simpler. This is because there is only one diagram (Fig
6.3
), which is quite similar to electron self-energy (Fig
5.1
), where the internal photon line is replaced by a gluon line. The one-loop quark self-energy in Feynman gauge with zero current quark mass can be written using Feynman rules defined in in subsec
6.1.1
as

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\textrm{q}}(P)\delta^{i}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-T\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{\{K\}}\,\,\,(-ig\gamma_{\mu}(t^{a})^{i}_{k})\frac{i\not{K}\delta^{k}_{l}}{K^{2}}(-ig\gamma_{\nu}(t^{b})^{l}_{j})\frac{(-i\delta^{ab}\eta^{\mu\nu})}{(P-K)^{2}}=-2g^{2}C_{F}\delta^{i}_{j}T\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{\{K\}}\not{K}\Delta_{F}(K)\Delta_{B}(Q),

(6.34)

where
Q=P-K
. We have also
used the identity
(t^{a}t^{a})^{i}_{j}=\frac{Nc^{2}-1}{2N_{c}}\delta^{i}_{j}=C_{F}\delta^{i}_{j}
with
N_{c}=3
and
\gamma_{\mu}\not{K}\gamma^{\mu}=-2\not{K}
. The superscript
q
corresponds to quark. After performing the frequency sum and
k
-integration as done in subsec.
5.2
, the quark self-energy becomes
[
5
]

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\textrm{q}}(P)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g^{2}C_{F}}{4\pi^{2}}\int kdk\left[n_{F}^{+}(k)+n_{F}^{-}(k)+2n_{B}(k)\right]\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\frac{\hat{\not{K}}}{P\cdot\hat{K}}

(6.35)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}C_{F}g^{2}T^{2}\left(1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{\pi^{2}T^{2}}\right)\left\langle\frac{\hat{\not{K}}}{P\cdot\hat{K}}\right\rangle_{\bm{\hat{k}}}=(m^{\textrm{q}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}\left\langle\frac{\hat{\not{K}}}{P\cdot\hat{K}}\right\rangle_{\bm{\hat{k}}}\,,

when compared with the electron self-energy in Eq. (
5.8
)
the only difference is overall group factor. So one obtains QCD results for quark from QED results of electron by replacing

e^{2}\Rightarrow g^{2}C_{F}\,.

In QED,
e
is the coupling constant and the group factor,
C_{F}=1
for
U(1)
gauge group whereas
g
is coupling constant in QCD with group factor
C_{F}=4/3
in
SU(3)
gauge group. With this one can transform electron thermal mass in (
5.7
) to quark thermal mass as

\displaystyle(m^{\textrm{q}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}=C_{F}\frac{g^{2}T^{2}}{8}\left(1+{\mu^{2}\over\pi^{2}T^{2}}\right)=\frac{4}{3}\frac{g^{2}T^{2}}{8}\left(1+{\mu^{2}\over\pi^{2}T^{2}}\right)=\frac{g^{2}T^{2}}{6}\left(1+{\mu^{2}\over\pi^{2}T^{2}}\right)\ \,.

(6.36)

We obtain the quark self-energy
[
5
,
50
,
65
]
by replacing the electron thermal mass
m^{\textrm{e}}_{\textrm{th}}

by the quark thermal mass
m^{\textrm{q}}_{\textrm{th}}
in Eq. (
5.8
) as

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\textrm{q}}(P)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(m^{\textrm{q}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{2p}\ln\left(\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}\right)\gamma_{0}+\frac{(m^{\textrm{q}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{p}\left[1-\frac{\omega}{2p}\ln\left(\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}\right)\right]\left(\vec{\bm{\gamma}}\cdot\bm{\hat{p}}\right)\,.

(6.37)

One can obtain
[
5
]
the structure constants
{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{q}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}^{\textrm{q}}
for quarks associated with the general expression for fermion self-energy from (
5.6a
) and (
5.6b
), respectively, as

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{q}}(\omega,p)\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{(m^{\textrm{q}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{p^{2}}\left[1-\frac{\omega}{2p}\ln\left(\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}\right)\right]\,,

(6.38a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\textrm{q}}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle\,=\,\frac{(m^{\textrm{q}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{p}\left[-\frac{\omega}{p}+\left(\frac{\omega^{2}}{p^{2}}-1\right)\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}\right)\right]\,.

(6.38b)

6.3.2  Effective quark propagator and collective excitations

Figure 6.4
:
The Dyson-Schwinger equation for the HTL effective quark propagator.

One can write the HTL effective quark propagator from (
2.63
) following Dyson-Schwinger equation in Fig.
6.4
in presence of a thermal medium
[
5
]
as

\displaystyle S^{\textrm{q}}(P)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\gamma_{0}-{\vec{\gamma}}\cdot\bm{\hat{p}})}{{\cal D}^{\textrm{q}}_{+}(\omega,p)}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\gamma_{0}+{\vec{\gamma}}\cdot\bm{\hat{p}})}{{\cal D}^{\textrm{q}}_{-}(\omega,p)}

(6.39)

We can obtain
{\cal D}^{\textrm{q}}_{\pm}(\omega,p)
by combining (
2.57
), (
6.38a
) and (
6.38b
) as

\displaystyle{\cal D}^{\textrm{q}}_{\pm}(\omega,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\omega\mp p)-\frac{(m^{\textrm{q}}_{\textrm{th}})^{2}}{p}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(1\mp\frac{\omega}{p}\right)\ln\left(\frac{\omega+p}{\omega-p}\right)\pm 1\right]\,.

(6.40)

The zeros of
{\cal D}^{\textrm{q}}_{\pm}(\omega,p)
in (
6.39
) define the dispersion and collective properties of a quark in the thermal bath. These properties are similar to those in QED as discussed in subsec
5.3
with the dispersion curves in Fig.
5.2
. Hence, we learn about the collective excitations in a QCD plasma from the knowledge gained from QED plasma excitations. One can also obtain the approximate analytic solutions of
\omega_{\pm}(p)
for small and large values of momentum
p
from
Eqs. (
5.11a
), (
5.11b
), (
5.12a
) and (
5.12b
). One can also obtain the spectral representation of
the effective quark propagator from (
5.4
) by replacing
m_{\rm{th}}^{\rm e}
by
m_{\rm{th}}^{\rm q}
. We want to mention here that the
{\cal D}^{\textrm{q}}_{\pm}(\omega,p)
obtained in Eq. (
6.40
) considering vanishing quark masses. In Ref.
[
78
]
, the dispersion relation of a strange quark is studied considering finite strange quark mass. Additionally, NLO quark self-energy and corresponding dispersion laws are studied in Ref.
[
79
]
in recent time.

6.4  Three-point Functions in HTL Approximation

6.4.1  Quark-Gluon three vertex

Figure 6.5
:
Two quarks-one gluon HTL effective vertex.

The one-loop approximation for the three-point quark-gluon vertex is shown in fig.
6.5
. The diagram (
a
) of Fig (
6.5
) is the free vertex as given in Eq. (
6.8
). The diagram (
b
) of Fig (
6.5
) is similar to the three-point QED vertex and can be written from the Feynman diagram as

\displaystyle-ig\delta\Gamma_{\mu}^{(b)}t^{a}_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\left[\left(-ig\gamma_{\alpha}\ t^{c}_{ii^{\prime}}\right)\frac{i}{\left(\not{K}-\not{P}_{2}\right)}\left(-ig\gamma_{\mu}\ t^{a}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\right)\frac{i}{\left(\not{K}-\not{P}_{1}\right)}\left(-ig\gamma_{\beta}t^{b}_{jj^{\prime}}\right)\frac{\left(-i\eta^{\beta\alpha}\delta^{bc}\right)}{K^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma_{\mu}^{(b)}t^{a}_{ij}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle 4\left(C_{F}-C_{A}/2\right)g^{2}t^{a}_{ij}\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}K_{\mu}\not{K}\Delta_{B}(K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{2}-K){\Delta}_{F}(P_{1}-K)\,,

(6.41)

where we used the relation
\left(t^{b}_{ii^{\prime}}t^{b}_{jj^{\prime}}t^{a}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\right)=-\frac{1}{2N_{c}}t^{a}_{ij}=(C_{F}-C_{A}/2)t^{a}_{ij}
and HTL approximation for external soft momenta
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
in the numerator.
Using the QED result from Eq. (
5.46
), we can write down
