) becomes

\displaystyle E\frac{dR_{i}}{d^{4}xd^{3}\bm{\vec{p}}}\!\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{{\cal D}_{i}}{(2\pi)^{5}}\frac{T}{4E}\,\,e^{-{E}/T}\,\,\int_{2k_{c}^{2}}^{\infty}ds\,\,\ln\left(1\mp e^{-\frac{s}{4ET}}\right)^{\mp}\,\,\int_{-s+k_{c}^{2}}^{-k_{c}^{2}}dt\,\,\,s\,\,\frac{d\sigma_{i}(s,t)}{dt}\,.

(8.39)

where it is evident that there is no phase space if
s\leq 2k_{c}^{2}
. We can now calculate
{\cal D}_{i}
for specific process as

\displaystyle{\cal D}_{qg\rightarrow q\gamma}=\underbrace{(2\times 2)}_{\mbox{spin}}\times\underbrace{2}_{q\,\,\&\,\,\bar{q}}\times\underbrace{(3\times 8)}_{\mbox{colour}}\times\underbrace{\left[\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{2}+\left(-\frac{1}{3}\right)^{2}\right]}_{\mbox{summed over}{\,\,u\,\,\&\,\,d\,\,}}=\frac{320}{3}\,,{\cal D}_{q{\bar{q}}\rightarrow g\gamma}=\underbrace{(2\times 2)}_{\mbox{spin}}\times\underbrace{(3\times 3)}_{\mbox{colour}}\times\frac{5}{9}=20\,.

(8.40)

After perforing
s
and
t
integrations the Compton differential rate for hard photon production in (
8.39
)
becomes

\displaystyle E\frac{dR^{qg\rightarrow q\gamma}}{d^{4}xd^{3}\bm{\vec{p}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\frac{5\alpha\alpha_{s}}{54\pi^{2}}\,\,T^{2}\,\,e^{-{E}/T}\,\,\left[\ln\left(\frac{4ET}{k_{c}^{2}}\right)+\ln 2+\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{\tiny{E}}+\frac{\zeta^{\prime}(2)}{\zeta(2)}\right]\,,

(8.41)

whereas the annihilation differential rate for hard photon production becomes

\displaystyle E\frac{dR^{q\bar{q}\rightarrow g\gamma}}{d^{4}xd^{3}\bm{\vec{p}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\frac{5\alpha\alpha_{s}}{27\pi^{2}}\,\,T^{2}\,\,e^{-{E}/T}\,\,\left[\ln\left(\frac{4ET}{k_{c}^{2}}\right)-1-\gamma_{\tiny{E}}+\frac{\zeta^{\prime}(2)}{\zeta(2)}\right]\,.

(8.42)

The total rate for hard photon production corresponding to physical processes (
2\rightarrow 2
) in
Fig.
8.10
is obtained
by adding (
8.41
) and (
8.42
)

\displaystyle E\frac{dR}{d^{4}xd^{3}\bm{\vec{p}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\frac{5\alpha\alpha_{s}}{18\pi^{2}}\,\,T^{2}\,\,e^{-{E}/T}\,\,\left[\ln\left(\frac{4ET}{k_{c}^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{3}\ln 2-\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{\tiny{E}}+\frac{\zeta^{\prime}(2)}{\zeta(2)}\right]\,.

(8.43)

which was obtained independently by Kapusta et al
[
99
]
and Baier et al.
[
100
]
.

If one uses Maxwell-Boltzmann (MB) approximation instead of Bose-enhancement or Pauli-suppression in the final state in (
8.35
) and following the same procedure
as before one gets the total hard photon rate
[
99
,
100
]
as

\displaystyle\left.E\frac{dR}{d^{4}xd^{3}\bm{\vec{p}}}\right|_{\text{MB}}=\frac{20\alpha\alpha_{s}}{9\pi^{4}}\,T^{2}\,\,e^{-E/T}\left[\ln\left(\frac{4ET}{k_{c}^{2}}\right)-\frac{1}{4}-\gamma_{\tiny E}\right]\,.

(8.44)

In all these above rates, there is a logarithmic infrared sensitivity, i.e. the rate diverges logarithmically if the soft cut-off
k_{c}\rightarrow 0
.
These rates agree with those reported earlier in the the literature
[
202
,
204
]
at least for the dominant logarithmic term. The constant terms are different if one uses different infrared regulator such as quark mass instead of soft cut-off on the four momentum transfer of the exchanged quarks in
t
and
u
channels. We note that the analyses in Refs.
[
202
,
204
]
no screening mechanism has been used as an infrared regulator. Instead the bare mass of the exchanged quarks,
m^{\text{q}}
in Fig.
8.10
has been used as a regulator but the rate diverges logarithmically if the mass of the exchanged quark is zero. Therefore, Kajantie and Ruuskanen argued
[
201
]
that the bare quark mass should be replaced by an effective thermal quark mass

m^{\text{q}}_{\text{th}}
. This means that even the production of energetic photons is sensitive to many-body effects of the QGP, since the exchange of soft quarks plays an important role in the production mechanism. A systematic treatment of
many-body effects is provided by the HTL resummation technique developed by Braaten and Pisarski
[
11
]
which was independently used in Ref.
[
99
]
and
[
100
]
to regulate the soft photon production from QGP. In the following subsection
8.2.3
, we will discuss the infrared regulated soft photon production from 1-loop HTL self-energy of photon.

8.2.3  Infrared regulated soft photon from 1-loop HTL approximation

In the previous subsec
8.2.2
for kinetic theory calculation, a cut of
k^{2}_{c}
on the four momentum transfer in
t
and
u
was considered to regulate infrared divergence which had forced to leave out a small amount phase space in photon rate obtained in (
8.43
) and (
8.44
) corresponding processes in Fig.
8.10
.

Figure 8.11
:
1-loop photon self-energy in HTL approximation. The internal line with black blob is the soft quark propagator in HTL approximation whereas that without blob is hard quark propagator.

We need to calculate the photon rate for this small phase space
[
99
,
100
]
for soft exchange of quarks which can be obtained from Fig.
8.11
.
In Fig.
8.11
, the dressing of one of the quark propagators is only required to get infrared regulated soft photon rate in lowest order in
\alpha_{s}

[
99
,
100
]
. It is not necessary to dress both the propagators or the vertices, as those will only introduce higher-order corrections in
\alpha_{s}
. Cutting through the blob (soft propagator) gives rise to processes in Fig.
8.10
.

The contribution of 1-loop HTL photon self-energy diagram in Fig.
8.11
can be written as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mu}_{\mu}(P)=-\frac{5}{3}e^{2}T\sum_{\{k_{0}\}}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{2\pi)^{3}}{\rm Tr}\left[\gamma_{\mu}S^{q}(K)\gamma^{\mu}S^{\text{q}}_{0}(Q)\right],

(8.45)

where it is summed up for two massless quark flavours
u
and
d
, and
S^{\text{q}}_{0}
is free quark propagator given in (
2.64
) and the HTL quark propagator
is
S^{\text{q}}
given in (
6.39
).
Using them and performing traces over Dirac indices, then BPY prescription
[
87
]
derived in (
A.15
), one can write
the imaginary part of (
8.45
) as

\displaystyle\textrm{Im}\Pi^{\mu}_{\mu}(E,p)=-\frac{10}{3}\pi e^{2}\left(e^{\beta E}-1\right)\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\omega_{1}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\omega_{2}\,n_{F}(\omega_{1})n_{F}(\omega_{2})\delta\left(E-\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}\right)\times\bigg\{\left(1-\bm{\hat{k}}\cdot\bm{\hat{q}}\right)

\displaystyle\times\Big[\rho_{+}(\omega_{1},k)\rho^{f}_{+}(\omega_{2},q)+\rho_{-}(\omega_{1},k)\rho^{f}_{-}(\omega_{2},q)\Big]+\left(1+\bm{\hat{k}}\cdot\bm{\hat{q}}\right)\Big[\rho_{+}(\omega_{1},k)\rho^{f}_{-}(\omega_{2},q)+\rho_{-}(\omega_{1},k)\rho^{f}_{+}(\omega_{2},q)\Big]\bigg\}\,.

(8.46)

In subsec
5.4
the free spectral functions
\rho_{\pm}^{f}
are obtained in (
5.13
) as
\rho^{f}_{\pm}(\omega_{2},q)=\delta(\omega_{2}\mp q)
whereas the HTL spectral functions
\rho_{\pm}(\omega_{1},k)
are also obtained in (
5.4
) which have pole parts involving
\delta
-functions given in (
5.14
) and branch
cuts given in (
5.15
). As discussed in the previous subsec
8.2.2
, an infrared cut-off
k_{c}
was introduced to regulate the soft phase space. Now, for Fig.
8.10
and Fig.
8.11
indicates that the exchanged quark should be dressed and must satisfy
[
99
]

\displaystyle-k_{c}^{2}\leq\omega_{1}^{2}-k^{2}\leq 0\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\Longrightarrow\,\,\,\,\,0\leq k^{2}-\omega_{1}^{2}\leq k_{c}^{2}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\mbox{and}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,E\gg T\,

(8.47)

We note that in the weak coupling limit
gT\ll k_{c}\ll T
, the arbitrary separation scale
k_{c}
cancels once the hard and
the soft contributions are added, as it should be the case for a consistent leading-order calculation
[
208
]
.
Now, one can perform the
\omega_{2}
-integration using the on-shell
\delta
-functions in the free spectral functions

\rho_{\pm}^{f}
, and then neglect the other energy conserving
\delta
-function
\delta(E-\omega_{1}+q)
as it will not be satisfied and
x={\angle\bm{\vec{k}},\,\bm{\vec{p}}}
. To perform the
x
-integration we change a variable
x\rightarrow q
as done earlier for obtaining the Born rate. Then performing the
q
-integration using the restrictions in (
8.47
), one gets

\displaystyle\textrm{Im}\Pi^{\mu}_{\mu}(E)=-\frac{5}{12\pi}e^{2}\,\left(e^{\beta E}-1\right)e^{-E/T}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}dk\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\omega_{1}\Big[(k-\omega_{1})\rho_{+}(\omega_{1},k)+(k+\omega_{1})\rho_{-}(\omega_{1},k)\,\Big]\,\Theta\left(k_{c}^{2}-k^{2}+\omega_{1}^{2}\right),\ \

(8.48)

We note that the pole contributions involving
\delta
-functions in the quark spectral functions
\rho^{\text{pole}}_{\pm}
will not contribute in this order but that of cut part
\rho_{\pm}^{\text{cut}}
, will do. Therefore, the physical process leading to the photon production from Fig.
8.11
is related to Landau damping, i.e. the interaction of a soft quark with thermal gluons. So, the diagram of Fig.
8.11
contains infinitely many quark-gluon loops as the HTL quark self-energy is resummed in the effective quark propagator in Fig.
6.4
.

We note that the
\rho_{\pm}^{\text{cut}}
has the complex momentum dependence, the integrations in (
8.48
)
cannot be done analytically
[
99
,
100
]
. However, using generalised Kramers-Kronig
relations, the so-called Leontovich relations
[
209
]
, it can be shown, that only the high energy limit of the HTL quark propagator is needed
[
210
]
and it can be performed analytically if one knows the quark response function. The result obtained
[
210
]
as

\displaystyle\textrm{Im}\Pi^{\mu}_{\mu}(E)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{10\pi}{9}\alpha\alpha_{s}\,\left(e^{\beta E}-1\right)e^{-E/T}\,\,T^{2}\,\,\ln\left(\frac{k_{c}^{2}}{2(m_{\rm{th}}^{\rm q})^{2}}\right)\,.

(8.49)

This agrees with Refs.
[
99
,
100
]
but the factor
1/2
under the logarithm was obtained numerically by using the full cut part of the spectral function. The advantage of
the use of the Leontovich relation is that it could be done analytically
[
210
]
if one knows the quark response function. Now, combining (
8.28
), (
8.49
) and (
8.25
), one gets the soft photon production as

\displaystyle E\frac{dR}{d^{4}xd^{3}\bm{\vec{p}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\frac{5\alpha\alpha_{s}}{18\pi^{2}}\,\,T^{2}\,\,e^{-{E}/T}\,\,\ln\left(\frac{k_{c}^{2}}{M_{\infty}^{2}}\right)\,.

(8.50)

Now, combining the hard part in (
8.43
) and the soft part in (
8.50
) one gets the energetic photon production as

\displaystyle E\frac{dR^{\text{KB}}}{d^{4}xd^{3}\bm{\vec{p}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\frac{5\alpha\alpha_{s}}{18\pi^{2}}\,\,T^{2}\,\,e^{-{E}/T}\,\,\left[\ln\left(\frac{4ET}{M_{\infty}^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{3}\ln 2-\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{\tiny{E}}+\frac{\zeta^{\prime}(2)}{\zeta(2)}\right]\,,

(8.51)

where the separation scale
k_{c}
that acts as an infrared cut-off of the hard part cancels out as claimed in Ref.
[
208
]
. This happens because the prefactor in hard and soft rate are same. So, the HTL approximation
[
11
]
works well here to regulate the soft part. We also note that apart from other factors the 1-loop HTL rate is proportional to
T^{2}/E
which is due to phase space. KB, in the superscript of the rate above, denotes the production rate of energetic photon by Kapusta et al.
[
99
]
and Baier et al.
[
100
]
.

It is worth noting that for finite quark chemical potential
\mu
, one cannot use the MB approximation for the initial particle distributions in the hard contribution as done in (
8.35
). Otherwise, no cancellation of the separation scale will take when the hard and the soft part are added. So, the photon production rate at finite
\mu
can be determined only numerically. For
\left|\mu/T\right|<1
, the factor
T^{2}
in (
8.51
) has to be replaced
[
51
]
to a good accuracy simply by
T^{2}+\mu^{2}/\pi^{2}
. The 1-loop HTL photon production rate has also been calculated for undersaturated (chemically non-equilibrated) QGP
[
211
,
212
,
213
,
214
,
215
,
216
,
217
]
.

Finally, we note that the result in (
8.51
), for realistic values of the strong coupling constants, is incomplete and the contributions beyond the leading logarithm (other factors inside the square brackets can be taken inside the logarithm) become important and can even dominate the leading order
{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})
coming from 1-loop HTL. Such contributions come from higher order diagrams, i.e., 2-loop diagrams in the HTLpt describing e.g. bremsstrahlung and inelastic pair annihilation, which have strong infrared and collinear sensitivity and contribute in
{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})
. They are not included in the soft part of the polarisation tensor using the full quark propagator (
6.39
), since they come from the exchange of a hard quark. In the following subsec
8.2.4
, we will discuss the photon production from 2-loop HTLpt.

8.2.4  Photon production from 2-loop HTL approximation

As discussed in the previous two subsections
8.2.2
and
8.2.3
, the
{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})
HTL rate in (
8.51
) were obtained by adding the 1-loop HTL contribution for soft quarks in Fig.
8.11
and the 2-loop diagrams in the right side of Fig.
8.9
, where the intermediate quark is hard. We further note that in the 2-loop diagrams in Fig.
8.9
, one assumed the exchanged gluon is also hard, because it is a thermal particle with average energy is
T
. However, if this gluon is soft, there will be a Bose enhancement factor
n_{B}(l_{0}\sim gT)\approx T/k_{0}\sim 1/g
and this contribution should be important. Therefore, according to the HTL approximation, one needs to replace the exchanged hard gluon in 2-loop diagrams in Fig.
8.9
by HTL resummed gluon as shown in Fig.
8.12
. This was first realised by Aurenche et al.
[
92
]
which was an important realisation.

Figure 8.12
:
Two-loop photon self-energy in HTL approximation where the exchanged gluon is soft but internal quark lines are hard. The dashed lines through the black blobs represent thermal cutting rules to obtain imaginary part.

Figure 8.13
:
Bremsstrahlung processes appearing from cutting through the black blob of the dressed gluon propagator in Fig.
8.12
.

The Bremsstrahlung processes in Fig.
8.13
come from the thermal cutting
[
45
,
165
,
166
,
206
]
through the black blob of the HTL gluon propagator in Fig.
8.12
, i.e., from the imaginary part of the gluon self-energy of the HTL gluon propagator corresponding to Landau damping of the time-like gluon. Since the HTL gluon propagator in Fig.
6.2
contains hard quark and gluon loops, which lead to physical processes contained in the imaginary part of Fig.
8.12
are bremsstrahlung in Fig.
8.13
and annihilation with scattering (inelastic pair annihilation) in Fig.
8.14
. Usually one expects that the imaginary part of 2-loop diagrams should be of the order
e^{2}g^{4}
due to additional 3-point QCD vertices and also multiplied by a factor
T^{2}/M^{2}_{\infty}
. However, due to a strong collinear singularity in the processes in Fig.
8.13
and in Fig.
8.14
there is collinear enhancement, powers of
T^{2}/M_{\infty}^{2}\sim 1/g^{2}
are generated so that they contribute at the leading order
{\cal O}(e^{2}g^{2})
in the strong coupling.

Figure 8.14
:
Annihilation with scattering (inelastic pair annihilation) processes appearing from cutting through the black blob of the dressed gluon propagator in Fig.
8.12
.

A) Two-loop rate by Aurenche, Gelis, Kobes and Zaraket (AGKZ)

The 2-loop rate calculation of Fig.
8.12
by Aurenche, Gelis, Kobes and Zaraket (AGKZ)
[
92
]
is very involved and tedious. However,
we now present the 2-loop final results of the production rates of photons (
E\gg T
) for the two processes originating from Fig.
8.12
.
The rate for the bremsstrahlung (brem) process for two massless quark flavours
[
92
]
is

\displaystyle\left.E\frac{dR}{d^{4}xd^{3}\bm{\vec{p}}}\right|^{\text{AGKZ}}_{\text{brem}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\frac{40\alpha\alpha_{s}}{9\pi^{5}}\,\,T^{2}\,\,\,\,e^{-{E}/T}\,\,\left(J_{T}-J_{L}\right)\,\ln 2\,,

(8.52)

whereas that for the annihilation with scattering (aws) process is obtained as

\displaystyle\left.E\frac{dR}{d^{4}xd^{3}\bm{\vec{p}}}\right|^{\text{AGKZ}}_{\text{aws}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\frac{40\alpha\alpha_{s}}{27\pi^{5}}\,\,E\,T\,\,\,\,e^{-{E}/T}\,\,\left(J_{T}-J_{L}\right)\,,

(8.53)

where the correct
8
8

8

We note that there was a numerical error in the computation of
J_{T}
and
J_{L}
in Ref.
[
92
]
that resulted in a factor of 4 enhancement of their values:
J_{T}\approx 4.45
and
J_{L}\approx-4.26
.
value of the constants were independently obtained in Refs.
[
218
,
219
,
220
]
as
J_{T}\approx 1.108
and
J_{L}\approx-1.064
. The total two-loop rate is the sum of (
8.52
) and (
8.53
). It can seen that 2-loop HTL rates in (
8.52
) and (
8.53
) is same order
{\cal{O}}\left(\alpha\alpha_{s}\right)
as 1-loop results in (
8.51
) due to the collinear enhancement by a power of
T^{2}/M_{\infty}^{2}\sim 1/g^{2}
as discussed earlier. On the other hand because of phase space the annihilation with scattering process is proportional to
T
where as the bremsstrahlung process is
T^{2}/E
which is same as that of annihilation and Compton process in 1-loop HTL. We also note that these HTL 2-loop photon production rates have also been generalised to chemically non-equilibrated QGP
[
221
,
222
,
218
]
.

However, the quantitative analysis of 2-loop rates
[
92
]
given in (
8.52
) and (
8.53
) were incomplete because a suppression of the emission rate due to the LPM effect
[
180
,
181
,
182
]
arise from multiple scattering during the photon emission process, which limits the coherence length of the emitted radiation, was ignored. Nevertheless, this was pointed out by the same authors later in Refs.
[
93
,
223
]
which had discussed the physics involved but did not attempt to make a complete calculation as claimed in Refs.
[
101
,
102
]
. That is, one internal propagator in the diagrams become nearly on-shell and receives
{\cal O}(1)
corrections from the quasiparticle width. This reflects sensitivity to additional scatterings which occur during the photon emission process. However, merely including the appropriate width on the intermediate propagator is not sufficient; a consistent treatment is required for a more elaborate and detailed analysis. This improvement were done over the rate of Aurenche et al.
[
92
,
93
,
223
]
by Arnold et al.
[
101
,
102
]
which we will discuss below.

B) Two-loop rate by Arnold, Moore and Yaffe (AMY)

As discussed above, Arnold, Moore and Yaffe (AMY)
[
101
]
provided a consistent method to take in account the
{\cal O}(1)
corrections by deriving an integral equation, similar in form to a linearised kinetic equation, whose solution determines the rate of bremsstrahlung and inelastic pair annihilation processes, to leading order in
g
.
In a companion paper
[
102
]
the accurate solution of that integral equation was obtained for specific theories of interest (viz., QED and QCD).

A full calculation of leading order
{\cal O}(\alpha\alpha_{s})
photon production rate, including 1-loop and 2-loop with fully considering the LPM effect in QCD, was obtained
[
102
]
as

\displaystyle\left.E\frac{dR}{d^{4}xd^{3}\bm{\vec{p}}}\right|^{\text{AMY}}_{\text{QCD}}=\frac{5\alpha\alpha_{s}}{9\pi^{2}}\,\,T^{2}\,n_{F}(E)\,\left[\ln\left(\frac{T}{M_{\infty}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{2E}{T}\right)+C_{2\leftrightarrow 2}\left(\frac{E}{T}\right)+C_{\text{brem}}\left(\frac{E}{T}\right)+C_{\text{aws}}\left(\frac{E}{T}\right)\right],

(8.54)

where

\displaystyle\lim_{{E}/{T}\rightarrow\infty}C_{2\leftrightarrow 2}\left(\frac{E}{T}\right)=\frac{2}{3}\ln 2-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\gamma_{\tiny{E}}}{2}+\frac{\zeta^{\prime}(2)}{2\zeta(2)}\,,

(8.55)

where
M_{\infty}
is given in Eq. (
8.25
). Note that the first three terms within the square brackets (along with pre-factors) in Eq. (
8.54
) are the contribution from the 1-loop HTL result and it agrees with Eq. (
8.51
) in the limit
E\gg T
. However, for arbitrary photon energy
E\gg gT
, the
C_{2\leftrightarrow 2}\left({E}/{T}\right)
,
C_{\text{brem}}\left({E}/{T}\right)
and
C_{\text{aws}}\left({E}/{T}\right)
involve multidimensional integrals which cannot be evaluated analytically but computed numerically. The fitted numerical results are summarised
[
102
]
as

\displaystyle C_{2\leftrightarrow 2}(y)

\displaystyle\approx 0.041\,y^{-1}-0.3615+1.01e^{-1.35\,y}\,,

(8.56a)

\displaystyle C_{\text{brem}}(y)+C_{\text{aws}}(y)

\displaystyle\approx\sqrt{1+\frac{N_{f}}{6}}\left[\frac{0.548\,\ln\left(12.28+1/y\right)}{(y)^{3/2}}+\frac{0.133\,y}{\sqrt{1+{y}/{16.27}}}\right]\,,

(8.56b)

where
N_{f}
is number of quark flavours. These fits are valid in the range
0.2\leq y\leq 50
.

The QED photon production rate based on Ref.
[
102
]
is also obtained
[
224
,
225
]
as

\displaystyle\left.E\frac{dR}{d^{4}xd^{3}\bm{\vec{p}}}\right|^{\text{AMY}}_{\text{QED}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\,\,T^{2}\,n_{F}(E)\,\left[\ln\left(\frac{T}{M_{\infty}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{2E}{T}\right)+C_{2\leftrightarrow 2}\left(\frac{E}{T}\right)+C_{\text{brem}}\left(\frac{E}{T}\right)+C_{\text{aws}}\left(\frac{E}{T}\right)\right]\,,

(8.57)

where
M^{2}_{\infty}=2(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\text{th}})^{2}
with
(m^{\textrm{e}}_{\text{th}})^{2}=e^{2}T^{2}/8
is obtained in (
5.7
).

We note that
C_{2\leftrightarrow 2}\left({E}/{T}\right)
is same in QED and QCD because the interference effect cancels out.
On the other hand the 2-loop coefficients are fitted in the range
0.2\leq y\leq 30
as

\displaystyle C_{\text{brem}}(y)+C_{\text{aws}}(y)\approx\left[\frac{2a\,\ln\left(b+1/y\right)}{(y)^{c}}+\frac{2\,d\,y}{\sqrt{1+{y}/{f}}}\right]\,,

(8.58)

where
a=0.374958
,
b=0.431304
,
c=-0.05465
,
d=0.157472
and
f=1.73085
.

Figure 8.15
:
The differential photon production rate as function of photon energy for 1-loop HTLpt (KB rate) and 2-loop HTLpt (AGKZ and AMY rates) at
T=0.25
GeV.

In Fig.
8.15
we have displayed the various photon differential rates: 1-loop HTLpt by KB given in (
8.51
), 2-loop HTLpt by AGKZ in (
8.52
) and (
8.53
)
and also by AMY in (
8.54
) for
T=0.25
GeV with 1-loop running strong coupling
[
226
]

\displaystyle\alpha_{s}(\Lambda)=\frac{12\pi}{(33-2N_{f})\ln\left({\Lambda^{2}}/{\Lambda^{2}_{\overline{\rm{MS}}}}\right)}\,.

(8.59)

For one-loop running we fix the scale
\Lambda_{\bar{\rm{MS}}}
by requiring that
\alpha_{s}(1.5{\rm{GeV}})=0.326
which is obtained from lattice
measurements
[
227
]
. For one-loop running, this procedure gives
\Lambda_{\overline{\rm{MS}}}=176
MeV and we consider the momentum
scale
\Lambda=2\pi T
. One can see that two-loop contributions dominate over one-loop result in all energies even though order of coupling
({\cal O}(\alpha\alpha_{s}))

is same both cases. This is because the leading
({\cal O}(\alpha\alpha_{s}))

is complete in two-loop calculations.

8.2.5  Discussion on photon production from higher than 2-loop

We have observed in previous subsec
8.2.4
that 2-loop HTL order contributes in 1-loop HTL order, i.e., in same order in coupling. The natural question arises what would happen if one goes beyond two loop. This question was also studied by AGKZ
[
223
]
have also studied by considering at 3-loop HTL diagrams and one such is displayed in Fig.
8.16
.

Figure 8.16
:
3-loop HTL photon self-energy.

Through power counting it is shown that the 3-loop diagram is proportional to the 2-loop diagram times a factor
g^{2}T/m_{s}
, where
m_{s}
is the infrared cut-off for the additional exchanged gluon. For longitudinal gluons the
m_{s}
is provided by the static electric screening of order
gT
. On the hand, for a transverse gluon this cut-off is provided by the non-perturbative magnetic screening mass of the order
m_{s}=g^{2}T
. Hence, the 3-loop contribution becomes the same order as the 2-loop. This
means that the infinite number of higher-order diagrams contribute to the same order,
\alpha\alpha_{s}
, as the 2-loop HTL diagram.This argument is basically in the same line that has been utilised by Linde
[
17
,
18
]
to indicate that the BPT in QCD breaks down as discussed in the subsec.
2.6
. We also note that the static magnetic fields are not screened at leading order of HTLpt since 1-loop HTL transverse gluon self-energy in all gauges vanishes in the infrared limit
\omega\rightarrow 0
, which has been shown in (
6.31b
).

However, the power counting argument can be used carefully because according to the Kinoshita-Lee-Nauenberg (KLN) theorem
[
228
,
229
]
, there may be cancellations of infrared singularities between different cuts of the diagram. We know that the sum over the different cuts, indeed, generates a kinematical cut-off. However, this cut-off becomes smaller than the non-perturbative static magnetic screening mass if the virtuality of the photon is small. In particular, for real photons the rate is always sensitive to the static magnetic screening mass. We now note that there is no magnetic screening mass in leading order HTLpt but if one considers the nonperturbative magnetic screening mass for transverse gluons then beyond 2-loop order infinitely, many higher-order diagrams contribute to the same order,
\alpha\alpha_{s}
, as the 2-loop HTL diagram. The real photon rate can be calculated using HTLpt that takes into account the screening of the chromoelectric scale but breaks down at 3-loop order due to Linde problem. On the other hand, however, in weak coupling limit
[
103
]
the dileptons with an invariant mass greater than
g^{2}T
, the KLN cut-off becomes relevant and their rate can be calculated perturbatively.

9  Mesonic Correlation Function

Although lattice calculations have achieved satisfactory precision in determining hadron properties in vacuum conditions, little is known about basic hadronic parameters in a thermal medium through such first principle calculations. For instance, masses and widths at finite temperature remain largely unexplored. Typically, lattice calculations of these quantities at zero temperature involve computing correlation functions in Euclidean time, a method that extends to spatial correlation functions at finite temperature. While spatial correlation functions indicate abrupt changes in medium hadron properties above
T_{c}

[
230
]
, they offer only indirect evidence for modifications in, for instance, hadron masses and widths.
A more suitable approach involves a detailed analysis of temporal correlation functions at finite temperature
[
231
,
232
]
, restricted to the Euclidean time interval
[0,1/T]
, with interesting information on hadronic states encoded in the spectral functions for these correlators
[
233
]
.

Current lattice calculations demonstrate significant changes in the behaviour of temporal correlation functions in the high temperature QCD plasma
[
94
,
192
,
193
,
231
,
232
,
233
,
234
,
235
]
. However, notably near the transition temperature
T_{c}
, the correlation functions clearly deviate from those of freely propagating quarks.Consequently, it is crucial to understand in how far the temporal correlation functions carry information about the existence or non-existence of bound states or resonances in the plasma phase. Various calculations within the framework of low energy effective models also suggest strong modifications of hadron properties
[
236
,
237
,
238
,
239
,
240
]
and consequently also
of the spectral functions
[
20
]
. However, it is difficult in such model calculations to deal with the quark substructure of hadrons, which will become important at high temperature where one expects to find indications for the propagation of almost free, massless quarks.
Eventually it is the hope, that spectral methods
[
241
]
, which have proven successful in characterising hadron spectral functions at zero temperature
[
195
]
, can also be extended at finite temperature. Particularly in the high temperature limit, well above the QCD phase transition temperature, it then might be appropriate to compare lattice calculations for temporal hadron correlators also with perturbative calculations. To some extent, non-perturbative information can be incorporated into such an analytic calculation by employing the HTL
resummation scheme
[
11
]
, which is successful in describing
many properties of hot and dense QCD matter
[
242
]
.

In this section, we will analyse
[
94
]
the structure of temporal correlation functions and their spectral functions within the context of HTLpt at zero spatial momentum. Indeed, the spectral functions, which one will extract from an analysis of temporal correlators, are closely related to quark-antiquark annihilation processes in the quark-gluon plasma. For the vector channel this is linked to the dilepton production at high temperature, which has been studied in the HTL-approximation
[
87
]
and discussed in details in subsec
8.1.3
.
The temperature dependence of the pseudoscalar correlator is related to the chiral condensate. Thermal effects on this as well as the pseudoscalar masses and dispersion relations influence the appearance or suppression of a disoriented chiral condensate which might lead to observable effects in relativistic heavy ion collisions
[
243
]
.

9.1  Generalities

9.1.1  Thermal meson correlation and spectral function

In this subsection we will present the framework for the calculation of thermal meson correlation functions and spectral function at finite temperature. The correlation function is constructed from meson currents

J_{M}(\tau,\bm{\vec{x}})=\bar{q}(\tau,\bm{\vec{x}})\Gamma_{M}q(\tau,\bm{\vec{x}})
,
where
\Gamma_{M}
is an appropriate combination of
\gamma
-matrices
that fixes the quantum numbers of a meson channel;
i.e.,

\Gamma_{M}=1
,
\gamma_{5}
,
\gamma_{\mu}
,
\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{5}
for scalar,
pseudoscalar, vector and pseudo-vector channels, respectively.
The two point function for a fixed momentum
\bm{\vec{p}}
is defined
[
231
,
232
,
234
]
as

\displaystyle G_{M}(\tau,\bm{\vec{p}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{\vec{x}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\langle J_{M}(\tau,\bm{\vec{x}})J_{M}^{\dagger}(0,\vec{0})\rangle{e}^{-i\bm{\vec{p}}\cdot\bm{\vec{x}}}\,,

(9.1)

where the Euclidean time is restricted as
\tau\in[0,\beta=1/T]
. Now, the thermal two-point functions in coordinate space,
G_{M}(\tau,\bm{\vec{x}})
,
can be written as
[
94
,
192
,
193
,
231
,
232
,
234
]
as

\displaystyle G_{M}(\tau,\bm{\vec{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle J_{M}(\tau,\bm{\vec{x}})J_{M}^{\dagger}(0,\vec{0})\rangle=T\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\int{{d}^{3}\bm{\vec{p}}\over(2\pi)^{3}}\;{e}^{-i(\omega_{n}\tau+\bm{\vec{p}}\cdot\bm{\vec{x}})}\;G_{M}(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{p}})\,,

(9.2)

and the Fourier transformed correlation function
G_{M}(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{p}})
is given at the discrete Matsubara modes,

\omega_{n}=2n\pi T
. The imaginary part of the momentum space correlator
gives the spectral function
\sigma_{M}(\omega,\bm{\vec{p}})
, following (
A.4
) and (
A.5
) as

\displaystyle G_{M}(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{p}})=-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{d}\omega\;{\sigma_{M}(\omega,\bm{\vec{p}})\over i\omega_{n}-\omega+i\epsilon}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\sigma_{M}(\omega,\bm{\vec{p}})={1\over\pi}{\rm Im}\;G_{M}(\omega,\bm{\vec{p}})\,

(9.3)

We note that for vector correlator the Lorentz indices are suppressed and can be denoted by
M=(00,ii,v)
corresponding to (temporal, spatial, vector). One can write the vector spectral function as
\sigma_{v}=\sigma_{00}+\sigma_{ii}
, where
\sigma_{ii}
is the sum over the three space-space components and
\sigma_{00}
is the time-time component of
\sigma_{\mu\nu}
.

Using (
9.2
) and
9.3
, one obtains the spectral representation of the thermal correlation functions in coordinate space at fixed momentum can be obtained as

G_{M}(\tau,\bm{\vec{p}})=\int_{0}^{\infty}{d}\omega\;\sigma_{M}(\omega,\bm{\vec{p}})\;{{\cosh}\left(\omega(\tau-\beta/2)\right)\over{\sinh}(\omega\beta/2)}\,.

(9.4)

We note that the Euclidean correlation function is usually restricted to vanishing three momentum,
\bm{\vec{p}}=0
, in the analysis of lattice gauge theory and one can write
G_{M}(\tau T)=G_{M}(\tau,{\vec{\bm{0}}})
.

9.1.2   Density fluctuation and its response

Let
{\cal O}_{\alpha}
be a Heisenberg operator where
\alpha
may be associated with a degree of freedom in the system.
In a static and uniform external field
{\cal F}_{\alpha}
, the (induced)
expectation value of the operator
{\cal O}_{\alpha}\left(0,\bm{\vec{x}}\right)
is written
[
244
,
245
]
as

\phi_{\alpha}\equiv\left\langle{\cal O}_{\alpha}\left(0,\bm{\vec{x}}\right)\right\rangle_{\cal F}=\frac{{\rm Tr}\left[{\cal O}_{\alpha}\left(0,\bm{\vec{x}}\right)e^{-\beta\left({\cal H}+{\cal H}_{ex}\right)}\right]}{{\rm Tr}\left[e^{-\beta\left({\cal H}+{\cal H}_{ex}\right)}\right]}=\frac{1}{V}\int d^{3}x\,\left\langle{\cal O}_{\alpha}\left(0,\bm{\vec{x}}\right)\right\rangle\>,

(9.5)

where translational invariance is assumed,
V
is the volume of the
system and

{\cal H}_{ex}
is given by

{\cal H}_{ex}=-\sum_{\alpha}\int d^{3}x\,{\cal O}_{\alpha}\left(0,\bm{\vec{x}}\right){\cal F}_{\alpha}\>.

(9.6)

The (static) susceptibility
\chi_{\alpha\sigma}
is defined as
the rate with which the expectation value changes
in response to that external field,

\displaystyle\chi_{\alpha\sigma}(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial\phi_{\alpha}}{\partial{\cal F}_{\sigma}}\right|_{{\cal F}=0}=\beta\int d^{3}x\,\left\langle{\cal O}_{\alpha}\left(0,\bm{\vec{x}}\right){\cal O}_{\sigma}(0,\vec{\bm{0}})\right\rangle\>,

(9.7)

where

\langle{\cal O}_{\alpha}(0,{\vec{\mathbf{x}}}){\cal O}_{\sigma}(0,{\vec{\bm{0}}})\rangle

is the two point correlation function with operators evaluated
at equal times. There is no broken symmetry as

\left.\left\langle{\cal O}_{\alpha}\left(0,\bm{\vec{x}}\right)\right\rangle\right|_{{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow 0}=\left.\left\langle{\cal O}_{\sigma}(0,\vec{\bm{0}})\right\rangle\right|_{{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow 0}=0\ .

9.2  Free Meson Spectral and Correlation Function

The starting point for a calculation of the meson spectral functions and the meson correlation functions is the momentum space representation of the latter
[
246
]
. In leading-order perturbation theory, one evaluates the self-energy diagram depicted in Fig.
9.1
(a),

Figure 9.1
:
The meson self-energy diagrams with free quark propagator vertices (a) and in the
HTL approximation (b).

where the internal quark lines represent a bare quark propagator
S_{0}(k_{0},\bm{\vec{k}})
. This propagator can be expressed in terms of its spectral function
\rho^{f}(\omega,\bm{\vec{k}},m)
and is conveniently written in Saclay representation as:

\hskip-28.45274ptS_{0}(k_{0},\vec{k})=-(\gamma_{0}\;k_{0}-\vec{\bm{\gamma}}\cdot\bm{\vec{k}}+m)\;\int_{0}^{1/T}\;{d}\tau{\rm e}^{k_{0}\tau}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\;{d}\omega}\,\rho^{f}(\omega,\bm{\vec{k}},m)\;[1-n_{F}(\omega)]\;{e}^{-\omega\tau},

(9.8)

where
k_{0}=(2n+1)i\pi T
, and the free spectral function

\rho^{f}(\omega,\bm{\vec{k}},m)={1\over 2\omega}\;\Big[\delta(\omega-\omega_{k})+\delta(\omega+\omega_{k})\Big]\quad,

(9.9)

with
\omega_{k}=\sqrt{\bm{\vec{k}}^{2}+m^{2}}
.
The thermal meson spectral functions are then obtained from (
9.3
), that is from the imaginary part of the correlation functions in momentum space,

G_{M}(\omega,\bm{\vec{p}})=2N_{c}T\sum_{k_{0}}\int{{d}^{3}k\over(2\pi)^{3}}\;{\rm Tr}\biggl[\Gamma_{M}\,S_{0}(k_{0},\bm{\vec{k}}))\,\Gamma_{M}^{\dagger}\,S_{0}^{\dagger}(\omega-k_{0},\bm{\vec{p}}-\bm{\vec{k}})\biggr]

(9.10)

In the case of free fermions this is easily evaluated. In the limit of vanishing external momentum one finds for the spectral functions,

\displaystyle\sigma_{M}^{f}(\omega,\bm{\vec{p}}=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{N_{c}\over 4\pi^{2}}\;\Theta(\omega-2m)\;\omega^{2}\;\tanh(\omega/4T)\;\sqrt{1-\biggl({2m\over\omega}\biggr)^{2}}\,\biggl(a_{M}+\biggl({2m\over\omega}\biggr)^{2}b_{M}\biggr)\quad,\quad

(9.11)

where different quantum number channels are characterised by the pair of parameters
(a_{M},b_{M})
. For the scalar (s), pseudoscalar (ps), vector (v) and pseudo-vector (pv) channels they are given by (-1,1), (1,0), (2,1) and (-2,3),
respectively
9
9

9

In the vector and pseudo-vector cases
we denote by
\sigma_{M}
the trace over the Lorentz indices of

\sigma_{M}^{\mu\nu}
.
.
In the massless limit the spectral functions are chirally symmetric,

|\sigma_{\text{ps}}|=|\sigma_{\text{s}}|
and
|\sigma_{\text{p}v}|=|\sigma_{\text{v}}|
.
In this case the remaining integral in (
9.4
) can
be done analytically and one obtains for example in the pseudoscalar case
[
246
]
,

\tilde{G}_{\text{ps}}(\tilde{\tau},\bm{\vec{p}}=0)=2\pi N_{c}\;(1-2\tilde{\tau}){1+\cos^{2}(2\pi\tilde{\tau})\over\sin^{3}(2\pi\tilde{\tau})}+4N_{c}\;{\cos(2\pi\tilde{\tau})\over\sin^{2}(2\pi\tilde{\tau})}\quad,

(9.12)

where
\tilde{\tau}=\tau T
and
\tilde{G}_{M}(\tilde{\tau},\bm{\vec{p}})={G}_{M}(\tilde{\tau},\bm{\vec{p}})/T^{3}
are the dimensionless quantities.

9.3  Meson Spectral Function in 1-loop HTL approximation

We now aim to move beyond the free quark approximation and explore in-medium quark propagators. In the weak coupling limit (
g\ll 1
), it is consistent to employ a HTL resummed quark propagator (and quark-meson vertex) when the quark momentum is soft, i.e., of order
gT
. A consistent way in the weak coupling limit (
g\ll 1
)
is the use of a HTL resummed quark propagator (and quark-meson vertex) if the quark momentum is soft, i.e. of order
gT
. These corrections to the spectral function are of the same order as the free spectral function for small energy
\omega
. Using the HTL resummation technique important medium effects of the medium such as the effective
quark masses and the Landau damping are taken into account.

As discussed in subsec
2.3.1
that the HTL resummed quark propagator is chiral symmetric in spite of the appearance of an effective quark mass
[
50
,
67
]
.
In the following we will ignore the parity of the meson states and will generically talk about scalar and vector channels
only. However, we will show results for pseudoscalar and vector spectral functions and correlators which in our notation are
strictly positive. To leading order one has to evaluate the HTL self-energy diagram shown in Fig.
9.1
(b).
The HTL resummed quark propagator is obtained in (
6.39
). We will discuss about the vertex for appropriate cases below.

9.3.1  Pseudoscalar meson spectral function

In the case of the scalar and pseudoscalar spectral function the
vertices are given by the bare vertices
\Gamma_{s}=1
and

\Gamma_{\text{ps}}=\gamma_{5}
, since contributions from a HTL resummation to
the vertices are suppressed in this case, i.e., lead to higher order
corrections, as discussed for the scalar case in the Yukawa theory
[
247
]

and scalar QED
[
248
]
. In the case of QCD the absence of HTL
corrections for scalar as well as pseudoscalar vertices has been shown
in the context of kinetic theory
[
249
]
.

In (
9.10
) we use HTL resummed propagator
S
from (
6.39
), pseudoscalar vertex
\Gamma_{\text{ps}}=\gamma_{5}
, performing trace over Dirac
matrices and then taking the imaginary part using BPY prescription in (
A.16
), one can obtain from (
9.3
) as
[
94
]

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\sigma_{\rm ps}(\omega,\bm{\vec{p}})\hskip-8.5359pt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359pt2N_{c}({e}^{\omega/T}-1)\int{{d}^{3}k\over(2\pi)^{3}}\;\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{d}x\,{d}x^{\prime}\;n_{F}(x)n_{F}(x^{\prime})\delta(\omega-x-x^{\prime})

(9.13)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359pt\left\{(1-\bm{\hat{q}}\cdot\bm{\hat{k}})[\rho_{+}(x,k)\rho_{+}(x^{\prime},q)+\rho_{-}(x,k)\rho_{-}(x^{\prime},q)]+(1+\bm{\hat{q}}\cdot\bm{\hat{k}})[\rho_{+}(x,k)\rho_{-}(x^{\prime},q)+\rho_{-}(x,k)\rho_{+}(x^{\prime},q)]\right\},

where
\bm{\vec{q}}=\bm{\vec{p}}-\bm{\vec{k}}
and the HTL resummed
spectral functions for massless quarks is given in (
5.4
) in the subsec
5.4
, in which

m_{\rm{th}}^{\rm e}
is to be replaced by
m_{\rm{th}}^{\rm q}
and have both pole and cut contributions.
Since the thermal meson correlation function has product of two spectral functions, they will receive pole-pole
(PP) and and cut-cut (CC) contributions as
functions

\sigma_{\rm ps}=\sigma^{\rm PP}_{\rm ps}+\sigma^{\rm PC}_{\rm ps}+\sigma^{\rm CC}_{\rm ps}\,.

One can obtain from (
9.13
) the PP, PC and CC contributions following subsec
8.1.3
and
quote below explicit expressions
[
94
]
of those to the pseudoscalar spectral function.

The pole-pole contribution (
\bm{\vec{p}}=0
) is given by

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\sigma^{\rm PP}_{\rm ps}(\omega)\hskip-8.5359pt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359pt{N_{c}\over 2\pi^{2}}\left(m_{{\rm th}}^{\text{q}}\right)^{-4}(e^{\omega/T}-1)\biggl[n_{F}^{2}(\omega_{+}(k_{1}))\bigl(\omega_{+}^{2}(k_{1})-k_{1}^{2}\bigr)^{2}{k_{1}^{2}\over 2|\omega_{+}^{\prime}(k_{1})|}+2\sum_{i=1}^{2}\bigg\{n_{F}(\omega_{+}(k_{2}^{i}))\bigl[1-n_{F}(\omega_{-}(k_{2}^{i}))\bigr]

(9.14)

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt\times\bigl(\omega_{-}^{2}(k_{2}^{i})-(k_{2}^{i})^{2}\bigr)\bigl(\omega_{+}^{2}(k_{2}^{i})-(k_{2}^{i})^{2}\bigr)\,{(k_{2}^{i})^{2}\over|\omega_{+}^{\prime}(k_{2}^{i})-\omega_{-}^{\prime}(k_{2}^{i})|}\bigg\}+\hskip 0.0pt\sum_{i=1}^{2}n_{F}^{2}(\omega_{-}(k_{3}^{i}))\bigl(\omega_{-}^{2}(k_{3}^{i})-(k_{3}^{i})^{2}\bigr)^{2}{(k_{3}^{i})^{2}\over 2|\omega_{-}^{\prime}(k_{3}^{i})|}\;\biggr]\,.

Here
\omega_{\pm}(k)
denote the quark dispersion relations for the ordinary
quark (+) and the plasmino (-) branch as discussed in Fig.
8.2
,

k_{1}
is the solution of
\omega-2\omega_{+}(k_{1})=0
,
k_{2}^{i}
and
k_{3}^{i}

are the solutions of
\omega-\omega_{+}(k_{2}^{i})+\omega_{-}(k_{2}^{i})=0
and

\omega-2\omega_{-}(k_{3}^{i})=0
, respectively. Note that for small momenta the last two
equations can each have two solutions. Furthermore,

\omega_{\pm}^{\prime}(k)\equiv({d}\omega_{\pm}(x)/{d}x)|_{x=k}
.

For the pole-cut contribution we find

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt\sigma^{\rm PC}_{\rm ps}(\omega)={2N_{c}\over(\pi m_{{\rm th}}^{\text{q}})^{2}}({e}^{\omega\over T}-1)\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!dkk^{2}\hskip 0.0pt\biggl[\Theta(k^{2}-(\omega-\omega_{+})^{2})\,n_{F}(\omega-\omega_{+})n_{F}(\omega_{+})\beta_{+}(\omega-\omega_{+},k)(\omega_{+}^{2}-k^{2})+\Theta(k^{2}-(\omega-\omega_{-})^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\times n_{F}(\omega-\omega_{-})n_{F}(\omega_{-})\,\beta_{-}(\omega-\omega_{-},k)\,(\omega_{-}^{2}-k^{2})\hskip-1.42271pt~+\Theta(k^{2}-(\omega+\omega_{-})^{2})\,n_{F}(\omega+\omega_{-})\,[1-n_{F}(\omega_{-})]\,\beta_{+}(\omega+\omega_{-},k)\,(\omega_{-}^{2}-k^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt+\Theta(k^{2}-(\omega+\omega_{+})^{2})\,n_{F}(\omega+\omega_{+})\,[1-n_{F}(\omega_{+})]\,\beta_{-}(\omega+\omega_{+},k)\,(\omega_{+}^{2}-k^{2})\biggr]\,.

(9.15)

Finally we obtain for the cut-cut contribution

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\sigma^{\rm CC}_{\rm ps}(\omega)\hskip-8.5359pt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359pt{2N_{c}\over\pi^{2}}({e}^{\omega/T}-1)\int_{0}^{\infty}dk\;k^{2}\int_{-k}^{k}dx\;n_{F}(x)\,n_{F}(\omega-x)\,\Theta(k^{2}-(\omega-x)^{2})

(9.16)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\biggl[\beta_{+}(x,k)\,\beta_{+}(\omega-x,k)+\beta_{-}(x,k)\,\beta_{-}(\omega-x,k)\biggr]\,.

9.3.2  Vector meson spectral function

The corresponding relation for the vector spectral function, which also includes HTL-vertex contributions,
is related to the dilepton production rate

{\sigma_{\rm v}}(\omega,\bm{\vec{p}}=0)=\frac{18\pi^{2}N_{c}}{5\alpha^{2}}\>\left({e}^{\omega/T}-1\right)\>\omega^{2}\>\left.\frac{dR}{d^{4}x\,d\omega\,d^{3}\bm{\vec{p}}}\right|_{\bm{\vec{p}}=0}\,.

(9.17)

The dilepton rate has been calculated in (
8.19
) following Ref.
[
87
]
in the HTL approximation and the
physical processes related to the pole-pole, pole-cut and cut-cut contributions have been discussed in details in subsec
8.1.3
. In particular, there are characteristic peaks that show up in the pole-pole contribution (van Hove singularities
[
172
]
) are related to the dispersion relation
10
10

10

In Refs.
[
177
,
178
,
179
]
it has been argued that the full in-medium quark propagator leads in general to two branches in the dispersion relation, of which one exhibits a minimum.

in plasmino branch in Fig.
8.2
. They are caused by a diverging density
of states which is inversely proportional to the derivative of the dispersion relations,
\omega^{\prime}_{\pm}(k)
. Owing to the minimum in the plasmino branch these derivatives vanish. We also note that such structures are also found in mesonic spectral function
[
94
]
. Apart from values close to the van Hove singularities in
\sigma^{\rm PP}

one finds that the cut contributions dominate the spectral function for small values of
\tilde{\omega}=\omega/T
, e.g. for
\tilde{\omega}\;\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$<$\kern-7.5pt\raise-4.73611pt\hbox{$\sim$}}\;g(T)
.

In Fig.
9.2
, we illustrate the pole
({\sigma}^{\rm PP}/T^{2})
and cut
({\sigma}^{\rm PC}/T^{2},~~{\sigma}^{\rm CC}/T^{2})
contributions to the scalar (Fig.
9.2
(a)) and vector (Fig.
9.2
(b)) spectral functions for
the scenario where
\tilde{m}^{\rm q}_{\rm th}={m}^{\rm q}_{\rm th}/T=1
, extrapolating the HTL results obtained in the
weak coupling limit to
g=\sqrt{6}
. Initially, it’s unclear whether the HTL resummation method can be applied to such substantial couplings, though extrapolation to realistic values of the coupling constant has been used for various QGP observables
[
115
]
. Nevertheless, this approach enables us to qualitatively explore the influence of medium effects, and as we will demonstrate, the minor corrections we observe validate our choice of couplings.

Figure 9.2
:
The pole-pole, pole-cut and cut-cut contributions to the
pseudoscalar (a) and vector (b) spectral function for
\tilde{m}^{\rm q}_{\rm th}={m}^{\rm q}_{\rm th}/T=1
.
The crosses show the free meson spectral function.

As depicted in Fig.
9.17
, the pole and cut contributions affect the spectral function differently. The former predominates for large
\tilde{\omega}
. The deviations of
\sigma^{\rm HTL}
from the free
spectral function in this energy range, along with the threshold for
\tilde{\omega}\simeq 2~\tilde{m}^{\rm q}_{\rm th}
, is due to the presence of a finite thermal mass in the quark dispersion relation, reflecting the nearly free propagation of two quarks in the plasma. Additional interactions of these quarks with the thermal medium (Landau damping)
are represented by the cut contributions, resulting in an enhancement over the free spectral functions for small
\tilde{\omega}
. Moreover, we observe that the pole-pole and pole-cut contributions to the spectral functions exhibit similar behaviour in the scalar and vector channels. However, the cut-cut contribution behaves differently at low energies. While it diminishes for small
\tilde{\omega}
in the pseudoscalar channel, it linearly diverges in the vector channel. This divergence can be traced back to the structure of the effective HTL-vertex, which contains a collinear singularity
[
250
]
. Consequently, infinitely many higher-order diagrams in the HTL expansion contribute to the same order
in the coupling constant
[
92
,
223
]
, indicating that the low-frequency part of the vector spectral functions is inherently non-perturbative.

In this context we note that the procedure used in Ref.
[
94
]
satisfactorily describes the soft fermionic modes, but its application to the propagation of hard quarks is not reliable. An improved version of the so-called next-to-leading approximation (NLA) scheme, which allows a better treatment of the hard fermionic modes, is proposed in Ref.
[
251
]
. The meson spectral functions have also been calculated in leading order HTLpt and beyond HTLpt for non-zero momentum
[
252
]
and chemical potential
[
253
]
.
We further note that the vector channel spectral function and the dilepton production rate from a QCD plasma at a temperature above a few hundred MeV are evaluated
[
184
]
up to next-to- leading order (NLO) beyond HTLpt but including their dependence on a non-zero momentum with respect to the heat bath.

9.4  Thermal Meson Correlators in HTL Approximation

9.4.1  Thermal pseudoscalar meson correlation function

We will now compute the temporal correlators from the spectral functions derived in the last section using (
9.3.1
). Although the temporal correlators are dominated by hard energies
\omega\sim T
, we use the spectral functions, calculated within the HTL resummation scheme, to account for medium effects from the quark propagator at least semi-empirically.

The interplay between pole and cut contributions to the HTL resummed spectral functions also impacts the behaviour of thermal meson correlation functions. The presence of a non-vanishing thermal quark mass tends to results in a more rapid decrease of the correlator in Euclidean time compared to the free massless correlator. However, the enhancement of the low-energy part, attributable to the cut contributions, counteracts this trend. This phenomenon is illustrated by the behaviour of thermal meson correlation functions in the scalar channel, as depicted in Fig.
9.3
for
\tilde{m}^{\rm q}_{\rm th}=1
and
2
.

Figure 9.3
:
The thermal pseudo-scalar meson correlation function in the HTL approximation for
\tilde{m}^{\rm q}_{\rm th}=1
(left) and
\tilde{m}^{\rm q}_{\rm th}=2
(right). The curves shown the complete thermal correlator (middle line), the correlator constructed from
\sigma^{\rm PP}_{\rm s}
only (lower line) and the free thermal correlator
(upper line).

As anticipated, the correlator constructed solely from the pole-pole contribution is steeper than the free correlator and is strongly dependent on scaled thermal mass
\tilde{m}_{T}
. Nevertheless, the cut contributions enhance the low energy contributions in the spectral function, thereby flattening the correlator once again. Surprisingly, for
\tilde{m}^{\rm q}_{\rm th}\simeq 1
this seems to compensate almost completely the deviations from the free correlator introduced by the pole contributions. The difference between the free and HTL-resummed correlators is most prominent for
\tau T\simeq 1/2
, where the contribution from the low energy regime in the spectral function is most significant. Conversely, as
\tau T
approaches to
0
and
1
, the free and HTL resummed correlators converge, as
\lim_{\omega\rightarrow\infty}\sigma^{\rm HTL}(\omega)/\sigma^{\rm free}(\omega)=1
.

9.4.2  Thermal vector meson correlation function

In subsec
9.1.1
, we have previously discussed that calculating of the vector correlators within the HTL method requires the use of effective quark-meson vertices, as illustrated in Fig.
9.1
(b). This results to a linear divergence of the spectral function in the vector channel at low frequencies, which in turn renders the temporal correlator infrared divergent. In fact, although the scalar correlation functions are infrared finite, it is to be expected, that also in this case the low frequency part of the HTL resummed spectral functions will be modified significantly from contributions of higher order diagrams. It thus seems to be reasonable to consider modified correlation functions, which are less sensitive to details of the low frequency part of the spectral functions. We therefore define the subtracted correlators

\Delta\tilde{G}_{M}(\tau)\equiv\tilde{G}_{M}(\tau)-\tilde{G}_{M}(\beta/2)~.

In the subtracted correlation functions the infrared divergences are eliminated. They are well-defined in the scalar as well as in the vector channels.

Figure 9.4
:
The subtracted thermal pseudo-scalar (left) and vector (right) meson
correlation functions in HTL approximation for
\tilde{m}_{T}=2
. The curves illustrate the complete thermal correlator (middle line), the correlator constructed from
\sigma^{\rm PP}_{\rm ps}
only (lower line), and the free thermal correlator (upper line).

In Fig.
9.4
we compare the HTL resummed subtracted correlation functions with corresponding results for the free case. This comparison reveals that following the elimination of the infrared divergent parts, the structure of the pole and cut contributions is similar in scalar and vector channels. Notably, the vector correlator appears to be even closer to the leading-order perturbative (free) correlator than the scalar correlation function. However, the primary distinction between the scalar and the vector channel using the HTL approximation lies in the differing behaviour of the cut-cut contribution to the spectral functions at low energies. Specifically, the vector spectral function diverges in the infrared limit, resulting in a singular expression for the vector correlation function. This feature is in agreement with the observation that the dilepton production rate, closely related to the vector spectral function, cannot be computed within the HTL improved perturbation scheme for small invariant masses of the order of
g^{2}T
.

9.5  Quark Number Susceptibility and Temporal Euclidean Correlation Function

9.5.1  Definition

The Quark Number Susceptibility (QNS) is a measure of the response of the quark number density with infinitesimal change in the quark chemical potential,
\mu+\delta\mu
. Under such a situation the external field,
{\cal F}_{\alpha}
, in (
9.6
) can be identified as the quark chemical potential and the operator
{\cal O}_{\alpha}
as
the temporal component (
J_{0}
) of the vector current,
J_{\sigma}(t,{\bm{\vec{x}}})=\overline{\psi}\Gamma_{\sigma}\psi
, where
\Gamma^{\sigma}
is in general a three point function. Then the QNS for a given quark flavour follows from (
9.7
) as

\displaystyle\chi_{q}(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial\mu}\right|_{\mu=0}=\left.\frac{\partial^{2}{P}}{\partial\mu^{2}}\right|_{\mu=0}=\int d^{4}x\ \left\langle J_{0}(0,\bm{\vec{x}})J_{0}(0,{\vec{\mathbf{0}}})\right\rangle\ =-{\lim_{\bm{\vec{p}}\rightarrow 0}}{\mbox{Re}}\ G_{00}^{R}(0,{\bm{\vec{p}}}),

(9.18)

where
G_{00}^{R}
is the retarded correlation function. To obtain (
9.18
) in concise form, we have used the fluctuation-dissipation theorem given as

G_{00}(\omega,{\bm{\vec{p}}})=-\frac{2}{1-e^{-\omega/T}}{\mbox{Im}}G_{00}^{R}(\omega,{\bm{\vec{p}}}),

(9.19)

and the Kramers-Kronig dispersion relation

{\mbox{Re}}G_{00}^{R}(\omega,{\bm{\vec{p}}})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d\omega^{\prime}}{2\pi}\frac{{\mbox{Im}}G_{00}^{R}(\omega,{\bm{\vec{p}}})}{\omega^{\prime}-\omega},

(9.20)

where
\lim_{\bm{\vec{p}}\rightarrow 0}{\mbox{Im}}G_{00}^{R}(\omega,{\vec{\mathbf{p}}})
is proportional to
\delta(\omega)
due to the quark number conservation
[
244
,
245
]
.
Also the number density for a given quark flavour can be written as

\rho=\frac{1}{V}\frac{{\rm{Tr}}\left[{\cal N}e^{-\beta\left({\cal H}-\mu{\cal N}\right)}\right]}{{\rm{Tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\left({\cal H}-\mu{\cal N}\right)}\right]}\frac{\langle{\cal N}\rangle}{V}=\frac{\partial{P}}{\partial\mu}\ ,

(9.21)

with the quark number operator,
{\cal N}=\int J_{0}(t,\bm{\vec{x}})\ d^{3}x=\int{\bar{\psi}}(x)\Gamma_{0}\psi(x)d^{3}x
, and
{P}=\frac{T}{V}\ln{\mathcal{Z}}
is the pressure and
{\mathcal{Z}}
is the partition function of a quark-antiquark gas. The quark number density vanishes if
\mu\rightarrow 0
, i.e., there is no broken CP symmetry. Now, (
9.7
) or (
9.18
) indicates that the thermodynamic derivatives with respect to the external source are related to the temporal component of the static correlation function associated with the number conservation of the system. This relation in (
9.18
) is known as
the thermodynamic sum rule
.

Owing to the quark number conservation the temporal spectral function

\sigma_{00}(\omega,{\vec{\mathbf{0}}})
in (
9.3
) becomes

\sigma_{00}(\omega,{\vec{\mathbf{0}}})=\frac{1}{\pi}{\mbox{Im}}G_{00}^{R}(\omega,{\vec{\mathbf{0}}})=-\omega\delta(\omega)\chi_{q}(T)\,.

(9.22)

Using (
9.22
) in (
9.4
), it is straight forward to obtain
the temporal correlation function
[
81
]
as

G_{00}(\tau T)=-T\chi_{q}(T),

(9.23)

which is proportional to the QNS
\chi_{q}
and
T
, but independent of
\tau
.

9.5.2  Leading order quark number susceptibility in HTL approximation

Now considering the fermion part of the HTL Lagrangian in Eq. (
6.77
)

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny QCD}}^{\textrm{\tiny HTL}}={\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny QCD}}+{\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny HTL}}=i\bar{\psi}\not{D}\psi+i(m^{\rm q}_{\rm{th}})^{2}\bar{\psi}\left\langle\frac{\not{K}}{K\cdot D}\right\rangle_{\bm{\hat{k}}}\psi.

(9.24)

We note that the covariant derivative usually contains background field or any source,
j
depending upon the physical requirement. To motivate the perspective we define the covariant derivative
D^{\nu}
as

\displaystyle D^{\nu}=\left[{\cal D}^{\nu}-i\delta^{\nu 0}(j+\delta j)\right]=\left[{\tilde{D}}^{\nu}-i\delta^{\nu 0}\delta j\right]\,,

(9.25)

where
{\tilde{D}}^{\nu}={\cal D}^{\nu}-i\delta^{\nu 0}j
. We note that
{\cal D}
contains gauge coupling
and
\delta j
is an infinitismal change in external sourceto which the response of the system can be calculated, as discussed earlier. Later it can be identified with a variation of some physical quantity depending upon the requirement of the system under consideration.
Now, expanding the HTL term in (
9.24
), we can write as

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny QCD}}^{\textrm{\tiny HTL}}(j+\delta j)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}\left(i\not{\tilde{D}}+\Sigma\right)\psi+\delta j\bar{\psi}\Gamma_{0}\psi+\delta j^{2}\bar{\psi}\frac{\Gamma_{00}}{2}\psi+{\cal O}(\delta j^{3}),

(9.26)

where the various N-point functions in coordinate space are generated as

\displaystyle\Sigma=(m^{\rm q}_{\rm{th}})^{2}\left\langle\frac{\not{K}}{iK\cdot\tilde{D}}\right\rangle,\,\,\,\Gamma_{0}=\delta^{\nu 0}\gamma_{\nu}-(m^{\rm q}_{\rm{th}})^{2}\left\langle\frac{\not{K}K_{\nu}\delta^{\nu 0}}{(iK\cdot\tilde{D})^{2}}\right\rangle,\,\,\,\Gamma_{00}=2(m^{\rm q}_{\rm{th}})^{2}\left\langle\frac{\not{K}K_{\nu}K_{\alpha}\delta^{\nu 0}\delta^{\alpha 0}}{(iK\cdot\tilde{D})^{3}}\right\rangle\,,

(9.27)

where these functions can easily be transformed into momentum space.

Now, the external source,
j
is considered as the quark chemical potential,
\mu
.
One can write the quark-antiquark partition function from (
2.39
) in presence of a variation of quark chemical potential
\mu+\delta\mu
as

\displaystyle{Z}[\beta;\mu+\delta\mu]=\int{\cal D}[\bar{\psi}]{\cal D}[{\psi}]\ e^{\int_{0}^{\beta}d\tau\,\int d^{3}x{\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny QCD}}^{\textrm{\tiny HTL}}(\psi,{\bar{\psi}};\mu+\delta\mu)}\,.

(9.28)

The pressure can be written
[
80
]
as

\displaystyle{P}[\beta:\mu+\delta\mu]=\frac{1}{\cal V}\ln{Z}[\beta:\mu+\delta\mu]\ ,

(9.29)

where the four-volume,
{\cal V}=\beta V
with
V
is the three-volume.
Expanding
{P}
in Taylor series around
\delta j
one can write

\displaystyle{P}[\beta:\mu+\delta\mu]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{P}[\beta;\mu]+\delta\mu\ {P}^{\prime}[\beta;\mu+\delta\mu]\Big|_{\delta\mu\rightarrow 0}+\frac{{\delta\mu}^{2}}{2}\ {P}^{\prime\prime}[\beta;\mu+\delta\mu]\Big|_{\delta\mu\rightarrow 0}+\cdots\cdots\ .

(9.30)

The first derivative of
{P}
w.r.t.
\mu
is related to the conserved density in (
9.5
) whereas the second derivative is related to the conserved density fluctuation in (
9.7
).

Now
{\cal P}^{\prime}
can be obtained as

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\left.\frac{\partial{P}[\beta;\mu+\delta\mu]}{\partial\mu}\right|_{\delta\mu\rightarrow 0}\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal V}{Z}[\beta;\mu]}\ {\int{\cal D}[\bar{\psi}]{\cal D}[\psi]\int_{0}^{\beta}d\tau\,\int d^{3}x\ {\bar{\psi}(x)}\Gamma_{0}[\mu]\psi(x)}\,e^{\left({\int_{0}^{\beta}d\tau\,\int d^{3}x\ {\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny QCD}}^{\textrm{\tiny HTL}}(\psi,{\bar{\psi}};\mu)}\right)}\,.

(9.31)

The full HTL quark propagator in presence of uniform
\mu
can be written as

\displaystyle{\cal S}_{\alpha\sigma}[\mu](x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\int{\cal D}[\bar{\psi}]\,{\cal D}[\psi]\,\psi_{\alpha}(x)\,{\bar{\psi}_{\sigma}(x^{\prime})}\,\exp\left({i\int d^{4}x\,{\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny QCD}}^{\textrm{\tiny HTL}}(\psi,{\bar{\psi}};\mu)}\right)}{\int{\cal D}[\bar{\psi}]\,{\cal D}[\psi]\,\exp\left({i\int d^{4}x{\cal L}_{\textrm{\tiny QCD}}^{\textrm{\tiny HTL}}(\psi,{\bar{\psi}};\mu)}\right)}\ .

(9.32)

We now note that this full HTL propagator,
{\cal S}[\mu]
, is indeed difficult to calculate and we would approximate it by 1-loop HTL resummed propagator (
6.39
),
S^{\textrm{q}}[\mu]
and also other HTL
functions below.

Now using (
9.32
) and performing the traces over the colour, flavour,
Dirac and coordinate indices in (
9.31
) one can write
[
80
]
the quark number density as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{P}[\beta,\mu+\delta\mu]}{\partial\mu}\Big|_{\delta\mu=0}=\ N_{c}\,N_{f}\,T\sum_{\{k_{0}\}}\!\!\int\!\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}{\rm Tr}\Big[S^{\textrm{q}}[\mu](K)\ \Gamma_{0}[\mu](K,-K;0)\Big],

(9.33)

where
`
Tr’ indicates the trace over
Dirac matrices,
N_{f}
is the number of massless flavours,

N_{c}
is the number of colours.

Similarly, we obtain
{P}^{\prime\prime}
as

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial^{2}{P}[\beta;\mu+\delta\mu]}{\partial\mu^{2}}\right|_{\delta\mu\rightarrow 0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-N_{c}N_{f}T\sum_{\{k_{0}\}}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}{\rm Tr}\Big[S^{\textrm{q}}[\mu](K)\ \Gamma_{0}[\mu](K,-K;0)\ S^{\textrm{q}}(K)\ \Gamma_{0}[\mu](K,-K;0)

(9.34)

\displaystyle-S^{\textrm{q}}[\mu](K)\ \Gamma_{00}[\mu](K,-K;0,0)\Big]\,,

where we have also used the following relation obtained
using the inverse of
S^{\textrm{q}}[\mu]
and the Ward identity as

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{\partial S^{\textrm{q}}[\mu](K)}{\partial\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-S^{\textrm{q}}[\mu](K)\ \frac{\partial{S^{\textrm{q}}}^{-1}[\mu](K)}{\partial\mu}\ S^{\textrm{q}}[\mu](K)=-S^{\textrm{q}}[\mu](K)\ \Gamma_{0}[\mu](K,-K;0)\ S^{\textrm{q}}[\mu](K)\ .

(9.35)

The quark number density follows from Eq. (
9.33
) as

\displaystyle\rho(\beta,\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{P}(\beta,\mu)}{\partial\mu}\Big|_{\delta\mu=0}=\ N_{c}\,N_{f}\,T\sum_{\{k_{0}\}}\!\!\int\!\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}{\rm Tr}\Big[S^{\textrm{q}}(K)\ \Gamma_{0}(K,-K;0)\Big].

(9.36)

Now, the Eq. (
9.34
) represents the quark number susceptibility (QNS) in the limit
\mu\rightarrow 0
as

\displaystyle\chi(\beta)\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\left.\frac{\partial{\rho}}{\partial\mu}\right|_{\mu\rightarrow 0}=\left.\frac{\partial^{2}{P}}{\partial\mu^{2}}\right|_{\mu\rightarrow 0}=-N_{c}N_{f}T\sum_{\{k_{0}\}}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}

(9.37)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\Big[S^{\star}(K)\Gamma_{0}(K,-K;0)S^{\star}(K)\Gamma_{0}(K,-K;0)-S^{\star}(K)\Gamma_{00}(K,-K;0,0)\Big],

where right hand side (RHS) is the temporal correlation functions at the external momentum
P=(\omega_{p},|{\vec{p}}|)=0
, which is related to the thermodynamic derivatives known as thermodynamic sum rule.The first term in the RHS of (
9.37
) is a
1
-loop self-energy whereas the second term corresponds to a tadpole in HTLpt with effective
4
-point HTL functions
[
82
,
84
]
as shown in Fig.
8.1
. We note that in the LO QNS, the second term in the RHS can be neglected as it contributes in higher order. However, we note that it is convenient to calculate the quark number density first and then the QNS by taking the
\mu
derivative of it. This will be equivalent to calculate the LO QNS directly from Eq. (
9.37
).

A) Free case

It is straightforward to obtain the quark number density for free case from Eq. (
9.36
), replacing HTL 2- and 3-point functions
S^{\rm q}
and
\Gamma_{0}
, respectively by free one and it becomes

\displaystyle\rho^{f}(\beta,\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{P^{f}}(\beta,\mu)}{\partial\mu}=\ N_{c}\,N_{f}\,T\sum_{k_{0}=(2n+1)i\pi T+\mu}\int\!\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}{\rm Tr}\Big[S^{\textrm{q}}_{0}\ \gamma_{0}\Big]\,.

(9.38)

Using free propagator
S_{0}^{\rm q}
is given in (
2.64
) and performing the trace over Dirac matrices, one can obtain

\displaystyle\rho^{f}(T,\mu)=2N_{c}N_{f}T\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\sum_{k_{0}=(2n+1)\pi iT+\mu}\left[\frac{1}{k_{0}-k}+\frac{1}{k_{0}+k}\right].

(9.39)

To perform the frequency sum, we use the standard technique of contour integration as discussed in subsec
2.1.3

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C}\,F(k_{0})\,\frac{\beta}{2}\mbox{tanh}\left(\frac{\beta(k_{0}-\mu)}{2}\right)dk_{0}=\frac{\beta}{2}\ \frac{1}{2\pi i}\times(-2\pi i)\sum{\mbox{Residues}}\ .

(9.40)

It can be seen that the first term of (
9.39
) has a simple pole at

k_{0}=k
whereas the second term has a pole at
k_{0}=-k
.
After calculating
the residues of those two terms, the number density becomes

\displaystyle\rho^{f}(T,\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2N_{c}N_{f}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\Big[n_{F}(k-\mu)-n_{F}(k+\mu)\Big]\,.

(9.41)

The QNS is obtained as

\displaystyle\chi^{f}(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left[\rho^{f}(T,\mu)\right]\right|_{\mu=0}=4N_{c}N_{f}\beta\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}n_{F}(k)\left(1-n_{F}(k)\right)=N_{f}T^{2}\ .

(9.42)

B) 1-loop HTL case

The HTL propagator
S^{\rm q}(K)
is given in (
6.39
) with (
6.40
). The zero momentum limit of the 3-point HTL function can be
obtained from the differential Ward identity
[
81
]
using (
2.62
) as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}(K,-K;0)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{0}}\left({{S^{\rm q}}^{-1}(K)}\right)=a\gamma_{0}+b\,\vec{\bm{\gamma}}\cdot\bm{\hat{k}}\ ,

(9.43a)

\displaystyle a\pm b

\displaystyle={\cal D}_{\pm}^{\prime}(k_{0},k),\,\,\,\,\,\,{\mbox{with}}\,\,\,\,\,\,{\cal D}^{\prime}_{\pm}=\frac{{\cal D}_{\pm}}{k_{0}\mp k}+\frac{2(m_{\textrm{th}}^{\rm q})^{2}}{k_{0}^{2}-k^{2}}\ .

(9.43b)

Now performing the trace over Dirac matrices in (
9.36
), the quark number density in HTLpt
[
81
,
80
]
becomes

\displaystyle\rho^{\textrm{HTL}}(T,\mu)=2N_{c}N_{f}T\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{\{k_{o}\}}\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[\frac{{\cal D}_{+}^{\prime}}{{\cal D}_{+}}+\frac{{\cal D}_{-}^{\prime}}{{\cal D}_{-}}\right]=2N_{c}N_{f}T\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{\{k_{0}\}}\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[\frac{1}{k_{0}-k}+\frac{1}{k_{0}+k}+\frac{2(m_{\textrm{th}}^{\rm q})^{2}}{k_{0}^{2}-k^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{{\cal D}_{+}}+\frac{1}{{\cal D}_{-}}\right)\right],\,

(9.44)

where
k_{0}=(2n+1)i\pi T+\mu
.
We note that the first two terms correspond to the free case whereas the
third term couples both HTL and the free contributions.
Apart from the various poles due to particles at the light cone
and QPs due to HTL in (
9.44
) it also has
LD part as
{\cal D}_{\pm}(k_{0},k)
contain Logarithmic terms which generate
discontinuity for
k^{2}_{0}<k^{2}
, as discussed earlier. Equation (
9.44
)
can be decomposed in individual contribution as

\rho^{\textrm{HTL}}(T,\mu)=\rho^{\textrm{QP}}(T,\mu)+\rho^{\textrm{LD}}(T,\mu)\ .

(9.45)

1) Quasiparticles (QPs) contribution

The pole part of the number density can be written as

\displaystyle\rho^{\textrm{QP}}(T,\mu)=2N_{c}N_{f}T\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C^{\prime}}\left[\frac{1}{k_{0}-k}+\frac{1}{k_{0}+k}+\frac{2(m_{\textrm{th}}^{\rm q})^{2}}{k_{0}^{2}-k^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{{\cal D}_{+}}+\frac{1}{{\cal D}_{-}}\right)\right]\ \frac{\beta}{2}\tanh\frac{\beta(k_{0}-\mu)}{2}dk_{0}\ .

(9.46)

1.

First two terms in (
9.46
) give the same contribution as the free case
in (
9.39
).

2.

The first part of the third term has four simple poles
at
k_{0}=\omega_{+},-\omega_{-},k,-k
. The last two poles (
k_{0}=\pm k
) due to hard
momenta are associated with the soft contributions and get easily
separated out.
After performing the contour integration,
its contribution can be written
[
80
]
as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C_{1}^{\prime}}\frac{2(m_{\textrm{th}}^{\rm q})^{2}}{k_{0}^{2}-k^{2}}\frac{1}{D_{+}}\frac{\beta}{2}\tanh\frac{\beta(k_{0}-\mu)}{2}\ dk_{0}=-\frac{\beta}{2}\left[\tanh\frac{\beta(\omega_{+}-\mu)}{2}-\tanh\frac{\beta(\omega_{-}+\mu)}{2}-\tanh\frac{\beta(k-\mu)}{2}+\tanh\frac{\beta(k+\mu)}{2}\right].

3.

The the second part of the third term has also four simple poles at
k_{0}=\omega_{-},-\omega_{+},-k,k
. After performing the contour integration its contribution can be written
[
80
]
as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C_{2}^{\prime}}\frac{2(m_{\textrm{th}}^{\rm q})^{2}}{k_{0}^{2}-k^{2}}\frac{1}{D_{-}}\frac{\beta}{2}\tanh\frac{\beta(k_{0}-\mu)}{2}\ dk_{0}=-\frac{\beta}{2}\left[\tanh\frac{\beta(\omega_{-}-\mu)}{2}-\tanh\frac{\beta(\omega_{+}+\mu)}{2}-\tanh\frac{\beta(k-\mu)}{2}+\tanh\frac{\beta(k+\mu)}{2}\right].

Using (
9.41
), and above two results in (
9.46
) one can obtain the HTL quasiparticle contributions to the quark number
density
[
81
,
80
]
as

\displaystyle\rho^{\textrm{QP}}(T,\mu)=2N_{c}N_{f}\!\int\!\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\Big[n_{F}(\omega_{+}-\mu)+n_{F}(\omega_{-}-\mu)-n_{F}(k-\mu)-n_{F}(\omega_{+}+\mu)-n_{F}(\omega_{-}+\mu)+n_{F}(k+\mu)\Big],

(9.47)

which agrees with that of the two-loop approximately self-consistent
\Phi
-derivable HTL resummation of Blaizot

et al

[
254
,
255
]
.

The QNS in LO due to HTL QP can also be obtained
[
81
,
80
]
from (
9.47
) as

\displaystyle\chi^{\textrm{QP}}(T)=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left[\rho^{\textrm{QP}}\right]\right|_{\mu=0}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!=4N_{c}N_{f}\beta\!\int\!\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\Big[n_{F}(\omega_{+})\left(1-n_{F}(\omega_{+})\right)\ +\ n_{F}(\omega_{-})\left(1-n_{F}(\omega_{-})\right)-\ n_{F}(k)\left(1-n_{F}(k)\right)\Big],

(9.48)

where the
\mu
derivative is performed only to the explicit
\mu

dependence
[
254
,
255
]
. Obviously (
9.48
) agrees exactly with that of
the 2-loop approximately self-consistent
\Phi
-derivable
HTL resummation of Blaizot
et al

[
254
,
255
]
. At very high
T
,
\omega_{\pm}\rightarrow k
and (
9.48
) reduces to free
case as obtained in (
9.42
).

2) Landau damping (LD) contribution

The LD part of the quark number density follows from (
9.44
) using (
5.15
) in the subsec
5.4

one gets
[
81
,
80
]

\displaystyle\rho^{\textrm{LD}}(T,\mu)\!\!=N_{c}N_{f}\!\!\int\!\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\!\!\int_{-k}^{k}\!\!{d\omega}\left(\frac{2(m_{\textrm{th}}^{\rm q})^{2}}{k^{2}-\omega^{2}}\right)\,\beta_{+}(\omega,k)\,\Big[n_{F}(\omega-\mu)-n_{F}(\omega+\mu)\Big]\ .

(9.49)

Note that we have considered
\beta_{-}(-\omega,k)=\beta_{+}(\omega,k)
when

\omega\rightarrow-\omega
. Also the LD part of the QNS becomes

\displaystyle\chi^{\textrm{LD}}(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\rho^{\textrm{LD}}(T,\mu)\right|_{\mu=0}=2N_{c}N_{f}\beta\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{-k}^{k}d\omega\left(\frac{2(m_{\textrm{th}}^{\rm q})^{2}}{k^{2}-\omega^{2}}\right)\,\beta_{+}(\omega,k)\,n\big(\omega\big)\,\big(1-n(\omega)\big)\ ,

(9.50)

where the
\mu
derivative is again performed only to the explicit
\mu

dependence. It is also to be noted that the LD contribution is of the
order of
m_{q}^{4}\equiv(m_{\textrm{th}}^{\rm q})^{4}
. The LD contribution can not be compared with
that of the 2-loop approximately self-consistent
\Phi
-derivable HTL
resummation of Blaizot
et al

[
254
,
255
]
as it does not
have any closed form for the final expression. However, the numerical values
of both the QNS agree very well.

C) QNS in conventional perturbation theory

In conventional perturbation theory, for massless QCD the QNS has been
calculated
[
46
,
256
]
upto order
g^{4}\log(1/g)
at
\mu=0
as

\frac{\chi^{\textrm{pQCD}}}{\chi_{f}}=1-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{g}{\pi}\right)^{2}+\sqrt{1+\frac{N_{f}}{6}}\left(\frac{g}{\pi}\right)^{3}-\frac{3}{4}\left(\frac{g}{\pi}\right)^{4}\log\left(\frac{1}{g}\right)+{\cal O}(g^{4})\ .

(9.51)

We note that for all temperatures of relevance the series decreases
with temperature and approaches the ideal gas value from the above,
which is due to the convergence problem of the conventional perturbation
series. Nevertheless, the perturbative LO,
g^{2}
, contribution is also contained in HTL
approximation through the
N
-point HTL functions.
